# BetterGrades Precalculus: Functions, Models, and Change

## Internal P0-P7 Exact Storyboard Manuscript

# Unit P0: Algebra and Function Readiness

**Governing question.** Which algebraic ideas must be immediately available before we can analyze functions with confidence?

This adaptive unit identifies the exact algebraic strands required by later function work and routes only the missed strands to repair.

## Unit lessons

## P0.1. Readiness diagnostic and routing

**Lesson outcome.** Use diagnostic evidence to identify which prerequisite strands are secure and which require targeted repair.

### Opening

A readiness diagnostic separates prerequisite strands so that a learner receives targeted repair rather than one vague total score.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use readiness diagnostic and routing to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Attempt each strand independently, compare reasoning as well as answers, route missed skills to a focused repair page, and use a different return check to confirm transfer.

The diagnostic is a routing tool, not a grade. A secure strand may be passed while another remains active for repair.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through readiness strand map, evidence-to-repair flow, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is treating the total percentage as a verdict instead of using the pattern of misses to select the next action.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** A learner solves every linear equation but cancels terms illegally in rational expressions.

**Solution.** Mark equation solving ready and route only the rational-expression strand to repair.

**Interpretation.** Different error patterns require different instruction.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Factor x^2-5x+6.

**Solution.** (x-2)(x-3).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses readiness diagnostic and routing in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** State the excluded input of 1/(x-7).

**Solution.** x≠7.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The diagnostic is a routing tool, not a grade. A secure strand may be passed while another remains active for repair.

Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Mark equation solving ready and route only the rational-expression strand to repair.

The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

### Check yourself

Classify 2(x-3)=2x-6.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

Identity; all real numbers.

</details>

### Practice

1. Classify 2(x-3)=2x-6.
2. Factor x^2-5x+6.
3. State the excluded input of 1/(x-7).
4. Evaluate f(-2) for f(x)=x^2+3x.
5. State the defining idea of readiness diagnostic and routing in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. Identity; all real numbers.
2. (x-2)(x-3).
3. x≠7.
4. -2.
5. A readiness diagnostic separates prerequisite strands so that a learner receives targeted repair rather than one vague total score.
6. The diagnostic is a routing tool, not a grade. A secure strand may be passed while another remains active for repair.
7. A frequent error is treating the total percentage as a verdict instead of using the pattern of misses to select the next action.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.
10. The next lesson, Equation and inequality repair, uses this result as part of a larger structure.

## P0.2. Equation and inequality repair

**Lesson outcome.** Classify and solve common equation and inequality families while distinguishing reversible steps from candidate-generating steps.

### Opening

Equation solving identifies inputs that make a statement true; inequality solving identifies an interval or union of inputs.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use equation and inequality repair to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Classify the family, state restrictions, use reversible steps when possible, label candidate-generating steps such as squaring or denominator clearing, and check candidates in the original statement.

Multiplying or dividing an inequality by a negative number reverses the order. Rational, radical, logarithmic, and even-power steps require domain checks.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through equation-family decision map, equivalence versus implication ledger, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reporting every algebraic candidate as a solution without checking the original equation.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve sqrt(x+1)=x-1.

**Solution.** The domain requires x≥1. Squaring gives candidates 0 and 3; only x=3 checks.

**Interpretation.** Squaring can enlarge the candidate set.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Solve |2x-1|=7.

**Solution.** x=4 or -3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses equation and inequality repair in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Solve 3^(x+1)=81.

**Solution.** x=3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Multiplying or dividing an inequality by a negative number reverses the order. Rational, radical, logarithmic, and even-power steps require domain checks.

### Figure storyboard

**P0.2-V1 - Equation-family decision map.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Solve sqrt(x+1)=x-1. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: The domain requires x≥1. Squaring gives candidates 0 and 3; only x=3 checks.

*Caption:* Equation-family decision map: visual evidence for classify and solve common equation and inequality families while distinguishing reversible steps from candidate-generating steps.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of equation-family decision map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.2-V2 - Equivalence versus implication ledger.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Classify the family, state restrictions, use reversible steps when possible, label candidate-generating steps such as squaring or denominator clearing, and check candidates in the original statement. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Equivalence versus implication ledger: visual evidence for classify and solve common equation and inequality families while distinguishing reversible steps from candidate-generating steps.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of equivalence versus implication ledger, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.2-V3 - Negative scaling reflection.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is reporting every algebraic candidate as a solution without checking the original equation. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Negative scaling reflection: visual evidence for classify and solve common equation and inequality families while distinguishing reversible steps from candidate-generating steps.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of negative scaling reflection, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

### Check yourself

Solve x^2-7x+12=0.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

x=3 or 4.

</details>

### Practice

1. Solve x^2-7x+12=0.
2. Solve |2x-1|=7.
3. Solve 3^(x+1)=81.
4. Solve 4-2x≤10.
5. State the defining idea of equation and inequality repair in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. x=3 or 4.
2. x=4 or -3.
3. x=3.
4. x≥-3.
5. Equation solving identifies inputs that make a statement true; inequality solving identifies an interval or union of inputs.
6. Multiplying or dividing an inequality by a negative number reverses the order. Rational, radical, logarithmic, and even-power steps require domain checks.
7. A frequent error is reporting every algebraic candidate as a solution without checking the original equation.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.
10. The next lesson, Factoring and polynomial structure repair, uses this result as part of a larger structure.

## P0.3. Factoring and polynomial structure repair

**Lesson outcome.** Recognize and apply the principal factoring structures needed for polynomial-function analysis.

### Opening

Factoring rewrites a polynomial sum as a product whose factors reveal zeros, multiplicities, and sign behavior.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use factoring and polynomial structure repair to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Extract a greatest common factor first, recognize grouping or special-product structure, factor each result again, and verify by multiplication.

A factorization is complete only over the stated coefficient system. Repeated powers may hide a quadratic-in-form pattern.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through factoring decision sequence, trinomial product grid, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is stopping after one factorization step or cancelling terms that are not common factors.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Factor x^4-5x^2+4.

**Solution.** Let u=x^2. Then (u-1)(u-4), so (x-1)(x+1)(x-2)(x+2).

**Interpretation.** Repeated powers can hide familiar quadratic structure.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Factor x^2-25.

**Solution.** (x-5)(x+5).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses factoring and polynomial structure repair in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Factor x^2+10x+25.

**Solution.** (x+5)^2.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A factorization is complete only over the stated coefficient system. Repeated powers may hide a quadratic-in-form pattern.

### Figure storyboard

**P0.3-V1 - Factoring decision sequence.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Factor x^4-5x^2+4. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Let u=x^2. Then (u-1)(u-4), so (x-1)(x+1)(x-2)(x+2).

*Caption:* Factoring decision sequence: visual evidence for recognize and apply the principal factoring structures needed for polynomial-function analysis.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of factoring decision sequence, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.3-V2 - Trinomial product grid.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Extract a greatest common factor first, recognize grouping or special-product structure, factor each result again, and verify by multiplication. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Trinomial product grid: visual evidence for recognize and apply the principal factoring structures needed for polynomial-function analysis.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of trinomial product grid, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.3-V3 - Factor-to-zero preview.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is stopping after one factorization step or cancelling terms that are not common factors. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Factor-to-zero preview: visual evidence for recognize and apply the principal factoring structures needed for polynomial-function analysis.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of factor-to-zero preview, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

### Check yourself

Factor 8x^2-12x.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

4x(2x-3).

</details>

### Practice

1. Factor 8x^2-12x.
2. Factor x^2-25.
3. Factor x^2+10x+25.
4. List zeros of (x+1)^2(x-5).
5. State the defining idea of factoring and polynomial structure repair in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. 4x(2x-3).
2. (x-5)(x+5).
3. (x+5)^2.
4. -1 with multiplicity 2; 5 with multiplicity 1.
5. Factoring rewrites a polynomial sum as a product whose factors reveal zeros, multiplicities, and sign behavior.
6. A factorization is complete only over the stated coefficient system. Repeated powers may hide a quadratic-in-form pattern.
7. A frequent error is stopping after one factorization step or cancelling terms that are not common factors.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.
10. The next lesson, Rational expressions and restrictions repair, uses this result as part of a larger structure.

## P0.4. Rational expressions and restrictions repair

**Lesson outcome.** Simplify and solve rational expressions while preserving every restriction from the original form.

### Opening

A rational expression is a quotient, so every original denominator zero is excluded even when a common factor cancels.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use rational expressions and restrictions repair to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Record restrictions, factor completely, cancel only common factors, combine over a least common denominator, and check equation candidates against the original domain.

A divisor rational expression must be nonzero. Cancellation is division by a common factor, not removal of matching terms across addition.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through legal versus illegal cancellation, original-domain overlay, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is erasing the original restriction after cancellation.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Simplify (x^2+x-6)/(x^2-4).

**Solution.** The result is (x+3)/(x+2), with x≠2,-2.

**Interpretation.** The cancelled factor at x=2 remains an exclusion.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Simplify (x^2-16)/(x-4).

**Solution.** x+4, x≠4.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses rational expressions and restrictions repair in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Solve 1/x=1/5.

**Solution.** x=5.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A divisor rational expression must be nonzero. Cancellation is division by a common factor, not removal of matching terms across addition.

### Figure storyboard

**P0.4-V1 - Legal versus illegal cancellation.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Simplify (x^2+x-6)/(x^2-4). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: The result is (x+3)/(x+2), with x≠2,-2.

*Caption:* Legal versus illegal cancellation: visual evidence for simplify and solve rational expressions while preserving every restriction from the original form.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of legal versus illegal cancellation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.4-V2 - Original-domain overlay.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Record restrictions, factor completely, cancel only common factors, combine over a least common denominator, and check equation candidates against the original domain. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Original-domain overlay: visual evidence for simplify and solve rational expressions while preserving every restriction from the original form.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of original-domain overlay, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.4-V3 - Candidate filter.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is erasing the original restriction after cancellation. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Candidate filter: visual evidence for simplify and solve rational expressions while preserving every restriction from the original form.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of candidate filter, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

### Check yourself

State restrictions of 5/[x(x-3)].

<details><summary>Check</summary>

x≠0,3.

</details>

### Practice

1. State restrictions of 5/[x(x-3)].
2. Simplify (x^2-16)/(x-4).
3. Solve 1/x=1/5.
4. Why can (x+4)/x not become 4?
5. State the defining idea of rational expressions and restrictions repair in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. x≠0,3.
2. x+4, x≠4.
3. x=5.
4. x is not a factor of the whole numerator.
5. A rational expression is a quotient, so every original denominator zero is excluded even when a common factor cancels.
6. A divisor rational expression must be nonzero. Cancellation is division by a common factor, not removal of matching terms across addition.
7. A frequent error is erasing the original restriction after cancellation.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.
10. The next lesson, Radicals and rational exponents repair, uses this result as part of a larger structure.

## P0.5. Radicals and rational exponents repair

**Lesson outcome.** Move among radical and rational-exponent forms, simplify exact values, and reject extraneous candidates.

### Opening

Even roots require nonnegative radicands and the principal square root is nonnegative, so sqrt(x^2)=|x|.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use radicals and rational exponents repair to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Extract perfect powers, convert between radical and rational-exponent notation, isolate a radical in an equation, raise both sides to a power, and check every candidate.

Negative rational exponents also require a nonzero base. Squaring is not reversible without sign and domain checks.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through principal root versus equation roots, radical-exponent equivalence, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is replacing sqrt(x^2) with x for negative inputs or omitting the final check.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve sqrt(x+6)=x.

**Solution.** Candidates are 3 and -2; only x=3 satisfies the original equation.

**Interpretation.** The domain removes the extraneous value.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Evaluate 16^(3/4).

**Solution.** 8.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses radicals and rational exponents repair in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** State domain of sqrt(3-x).

**Solution.** x≤3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Negative rational exponents also require a nonzero base. Squaring is not reversible without sign and domain checks.

### Figure storyboard

**P0.5-V1 - Principal root versus equation roots.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Solve sqrt(x+6)=x. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Candidates are 3 and -2; only x=3 satisfies the original equation.

*Caption:* Principal root versus equation roots: visual evidence for move among radical and rational-exponent forms, simplify exact values, and reject extraneous candidates.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of principal root versus equation roots, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.5-V2 - Radical-exponent equivalence.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Extract perfect powers, convert between radical and rational-exponent notation, isolate a radical in an equation, raise both sides to a power, and check every candidate. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Radical-exponent equivalence: visual evidence for move among radical and rational-exponent forms, simplify exact values, and reject extraneous candidates.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of radical-exponent equivalence, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.5-V3 - Extraneous-solution map.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is replacing sqrt(x^2) with x for negative inputs or omitting the final check. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Extraneous-solution map: visual evidence for move among radical and rational-exponent forms, simplify exact values, and reject extraneous candidates.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of extraneous-solution map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

### Check yourself

Simplify sqrt(48).

<details><summary>Check</summary>

4sqrt(3).

</details>

### Practice

1. Simplify sqrt(48).
2. Evaluate 16^(3/4).
3. State domain of sqrt(3-x).
4. Simplify sqrt(x^2).
5. State the defining idea of radicals and rational exponents repair in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. 4sqrt(3).
2. 8.
3. x≤3.
4. |x|.
5. Even roots require nonnegative radicands and the principal square root is nonnegative, so sqrt(x^2)=|x|.
6. Negative rational exponents also require a nonzero base. Squaring is not reversible without sign and domain checks.
7. A frequent error is replacing sqrt(x^2) with x for negative inputs or omitting the final check.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P0.6. Exponential and logarithmic algebra repair

**Lesson outcome.** Apply exponent and logarithm laws, convert between inverse forms, and solve basic equations with valid domains.

### Opening

Exponent laws describe repeated multiplication, while logarithms reverse exponentiation.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use exponential and logarithmic algebra repair to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Apply product, quotient, and power laws only to multiplicative structure; convert log_b(y)=x and b^x=y without changing the base-exponent-output roles.

Real logarithm arguments must be positive, and bases must be positive and unequal to 1.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through exponent-law derivation, exponential-log inverse pair, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is applying a product law to a sum, such as log(a+b)=log a+log b.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Evaluate log_3(1/27).

**Solution.** -3, because 3^(-3)=1/27.

**Interpretation.** A logarithm reports an exponent.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Evaluate log_5(125).

**Solution.** 3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses exponential and logarithmic algebra repair in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Expand log_b(xy^2).

**Solution.** log_b x+2log_b y.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Real logarithm arguments must be positive, and bases must be positive and unequal to 1.

### Figure storyboard

**P0.6-V1 - Exponent-law derivation.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Evaluate log_3(1/27). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: -3, because 3^(-3)=1/27.

*Caption:* Exponent-law derivation: visual evidence for apply exponent and logarithm laws, convert between inverse forms, and solve basic equations with valid domains.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of exponent-law derivation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P0.7. Function notation and graph reading repair

**Lesson outcome.** Evaluate functions, solve output conditions, and read domain, range, intercepts, extrema, and intervals from graphs.

### Opening

Function notation f(a) asks for one output, while f(x)=k asks for every input that produces the output k.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use function notation and graph reading repair to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Use vertical tracing for evaluation, horizontal tracing for solving, and interval notation for domain, range, and behavior.

Open circles exclude points, arrows indicate continuation, and intervals of increase or decrease are reported with input values.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through evaluate-versus-solve probes, graph-feature atlas, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reading f(3) as multiplication or reporting output values where input intervals are required.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** If f(x)=x^2, solve f(x)=9.

**Solution.** x=±3.

**Interpretation.** A fixed output may have several preimages.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** State range with minimum -2 and no upper bound.

**Solution.** [-2,∞).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses function notation and graph reading repair in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Meaning of an open circle at (3,5).

**Solution.** The point is excluded.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Open circles exclude points, arrows indicate continuation, and intervals of increase or decrease are reported with input values.

### Figure storyboard

**P0.7-V2 - Graph-feature atlas.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Use vertical tracing for evaluation, horizontal tracing for solving, and interval notation for domain, range, and behavior. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Graph-feature atlas: visual evidence for evaluate functions, solve output conditions, and read domain, range, intercepts, extrema, and intervals from graphs.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of graph-feature atlas, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P0.8. Mixed readiness synthesis

**Lesson outcome.** Select methods across prerequisite strands, justify restrictions, and communicate exact and approximate results.

### Opening

A complete solution identifies the mathematical object, states restrictions, selects a method, carries out the work, and interprets the result.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use mixed readiness synthesis to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use signed arithmetic accurately.
2. Show algebraic steps in a checkable order.
3. State restrictions before simplifying.

### Exact learner exposition

Retain exact form until approximation is needed, verify the result in the original relation, and state the evidence interval or graph window supporting numerical claims.

A correct calculation can still be an invalid model conclusion when units, domain, or assumptions are ignored.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through complete-solution workflow, exact versus approximate forms, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reporting a calculator output without explaining why the method applies or what the number means.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Find domain and zero of sqrt(x-1)/(x-4).

**Solution.** Domain [1,4)∪(4,∞); zero x=1.

**Interpretation.** The domain both defines the function and validates its zero.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Average rate of x^2 from 1 to 4.

**Solution.** 5.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses mixed readiness synthesis in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** What does a polynomial sign change guarantee?

**Solution.** At least one zero in the interval.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A correct calculation can still be an invalid model conclusion when units, domain, or assumptions are ignored.

### Figure storyboard

**P0.8-V1 - Complete-solution workflow.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Find domain and zero of sqrt(x-1)/(x-4). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Domain [1,4)∪(4,∞); zero x=1.

*Caption:* Complete-solution workflow: visual evidence for select methods across prerequisite strands, justify restrictions, and communicate exact and approximate results.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of complete-solution workflow, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.8-V2 - Exact versus approximate forms.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Retain exact form until approximation is needed, verify the result in the original relation, and state the evidence interval or graph window supporting numerical claims. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Exact versus approximate forms: visual evidence for select methods across prerequisite strands, justify restrictions, and communicate exact and approximate results.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of exact versus approximate forms, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P0.8-V3 - Method-selection network.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is reporting a calculator output without explaining why the method applies or what the number means. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Method-selection network: visual evidence for select methods across prerequisite strands, justify restrictions, and communicate exact and approximate results.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of method-selection network, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

</details>

### Practice

2. Average rate of x^2 from 1 to 4.
3. What does a polynomial sign change guarantee?
4. One valid use of graphing technology.
5. State the defining idea of mixed readiness synthesis in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. 5.
3. At least one zero in the interval.
4. Bracket or approximate a root on a stated interval.

6. A correct calculation can still be an invalid model conclusion when units, domain, or assumptions are ignored.

## Unit investigation

The investigation for Algebra and Function Readiness combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

## Unit review and mastery

The review mixes current-unit questions with retrieval from earlier units. The mastery assessment balances symbolic fluency, graph and table interpretation, explanation, error analysis, and modeling transfer.


\pagebreak

# Unit P1: Functions and Multiple Representations

**Governing question.** What is a function, and what does each representation allow us to see?

This unit establishes the language of functions across quantities, words, tables, graphs, formulas, and models.

## Unit lessons

## P1.1. Quantities, variables, and dependency

**Lesson outcome.** Identify the input, output, parameters, and units in a quantitative relationship and explain how the quantities depend on one another.

### Opening

An input is supplied to a relationship, an output is determined by it, and parameters remain fixed for one selected model.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use quantities, variables, and dependency to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Define every quantity and unit, identify the direction of dependency, and check that terms combined in a formula have compatible units.

The same symbol can play different roles in different models; the context determines whether it is input, output, or parameter.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through quantity dependency map, parameter family graph, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is manipulating symbols before defining what they measure.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** A rental costs $18 plus $7 per hour.

**Solution.** C(h)=18+7h dollars.

**Interpretation.** The fixed fee and hourly rate have different parameter meanings.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Units of 60 in d(t)=60t miles.

**Solution.** Miles per hour.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses quantities, variables, and dependency in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Perimeter with width 5 and length L.

**Solution.** 2L+10.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The same symbol can play different roles in different models; the context determines whether it is input, output, or parameter.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.1-V1 - Quantity dependency map.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: A rental costs $18 plus $7 per hour. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: C(h)=18+7h dollars.

*Caption:* Quantity dependency map: visual evidence for identify the input, output, parameters, and units in a quantitative relationship and explain how the quantities depend on one another.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of quantity dependency map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.1-V2 - Parameter family graph.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Define every quantity and unit, identify the direction of dependency, and check that terms combined in a formula have compatible units. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Parameter family graph: visual evidence for identify the input, output, parameters, and units in a quantitative relationship and explain how the quantities depend on one another.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of parameter family graph, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.1-V3 - Unit consistency ledger.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is manipulating symbols before defining what they measure. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Unit consistency ledger: visual evidence for identify the input, output, parameters, and units in a quantitative relationship and explain how the quantities depend on one another.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of unit consistency ledger, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

C(m)=9+2m.

</details>

### Practice

2. Units of 60 in d(t)=60t miles.
3. Perimeter with width 5 and length L.
4. Horizontal axis for cost as function of hours.
5. State the defining idea of quantities, variables, and dependency in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. C(m)=9+2m.
2. Miles per hour.
3. 2L+10.
4. Hours.
5. An input is supplied to a relationship, an output is determined by it, and parameters remain fixed for one selected model.
6. The same symbol can play different roles in different models; the context determines whether it is input, output, or parameter.
7. A frequent error is manipulating symbols before defining what they measure.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P1.2. Relations and functions

**Lesson outcome.** Determine whether a relation is a function by applying the rule that each allowed input has exactly one output.

### Opening

A relation is a function when every allowed input has exactly one output. Several inputs may share an output.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use relations and functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Check repeated inputs in a table, outgoing arrows in a mapping, vertical intersections in a graph, or the rule stated by a context.

The function condition applies only to inputs in the domain. One-to-one behavior is a separate stronger property.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through mapping comparison, vertical-line derivation, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is rejecting a many-to-one function because an output repeats.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Does (0,3),(1,5),(2,5),(3,8) define a function?

**Solution.** Yes. Every input has one output.

**Interpretation.** Repeated output 5 is allowed.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Is y=x^2 a function of x?

**Solution.** Yes.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses relations and functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Give a function not one-to-one.

**Solution.** For example y=x^2.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The function condition applies only to inputs in the domain. One-to-one behavior is a separate stronger property.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.2-V1 - Mapping comparison.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Does (0,3),(1,5),(2,5),(3,8) define a function? Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Yes. Every input has one output.

*Caption:* Mapping comparison: visual evidence for determine whether a relation is a function by applying the rule that each allowed input has exactly one output.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of mapping comparison, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.2-V2 - Vertical-line derivation.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Check repeated inputs in a table, outgoing arrows in a mapping, vertical intersections in a graph, or the rule stated by a context. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Vertical-line derivation: visual evidence for determine whether a relation is a function by applying the rule that each allowed input has exactly one output.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of vertical-line derivation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.2-V3 - Discrete and continuous functions.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is rejecting a many-to-one function because an output repeats. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Discrete and continuous functions: visual evidence for determine whether a relation is a function by applying the rule that each allowed input has exactly one output.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of discrete and continuous functions, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

No.

</details>

### Practice

2. Is y=x^2 a function of x?
3. Give a function not one-to-one.
4. Why does a vertical line fail as a function of x?
5. State the defining idea of relations and functions in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. Yes.
3. For example y=x^2.
4. One input has many outputs.
5. A relation is a function when every allowed input has exactly one output. Several inputs may share an output.
6. The function condition applies only to inputs in the domain. One-to-one behavior is a separate stronger property.
7. A frequent error is rejecting a many-to-one function because an output repeats.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P1.3. Function notation, evaluation, and solving

**Lesson outcome.** Use function notation accurately and distinguish finding an output from finding inputs that produce a chosen output.

### Opening

The notation f(x) names the output of function f at input x; it is not multiplication.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use function notation, evaluation, and solving to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Substitute the entire input with parentheses or trace the input through a table or graph. For f(x)=k, solve for every preimage.

A function produces at most one output for an allowed input, but a fixed output may have several inputs.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through vertical versus horizontal tracing, substitution grouping, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is confusing f(a) with the equation f(x)=a.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** For f(x)=2x^2-3x, find f(-2).

**Solution.** 14.

**Interpretation.** Parentheses preserve the negative input.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** For g(x)=x^2+1, find g(-3).

**Solution.** 10.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses function notation, evaluation, and solving in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** For f(x)=x^2, solve f(x)=16.

**Solution.** x=±4.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A function produces at most one output for an allowed input, but a fixed output may have several inputs.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.3-V1 - Vertical versus horizontal tracing.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: For f(x)=2x^2-3x, find f(-2). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: 14.

*Caption:* Vertical versus horizontal tracing: visual evidence for use function notation accurately and distinguish finding an output from finding inputs that produce a chosen output.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of vertical versus horizontal tracing, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.3-V2 - Substitution grouping.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Substitute the entire input with parentheses or trace the input through a table or graph. For f(x)=k, solve for every preimage. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Substitution grouping: visual evidence for use function notation accurately and distinguish finding an output from finding inputs that produce a chosen output.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of substitution grouping, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.3-V3 - Preimage set.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is confusing f(a) with the equation f(x)=a. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Preimage set: visual evidence for use function notation accurately and distinguish finding an output from finding inputs that produce a chosen output.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of preimage set, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

</details>

10. State how this lesson connects forward.

6. A function produces at most one output for an allowed input, but a fixed output may have several inputs.
7. A frequent error is confusing f(a) with the equation f(x)=a.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.

## P1.4. Words, tables, graphs, and formulas

**Lesson outcome.** Translate a function relationship among verbal, tabular, graphical, and algebraic representations while preserving quantities, units, and domain.

### Opening

Words define quantities and assumptions, tables show selected pairs, graphs reveal global behavior, and formulas support exact calculation.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use words, tables, graphs, and formulas to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Translate one representation at a time while preserving input, output, units, and domain; then verify agreement at shared values.

Connecting data points claims that intermediate inputs are meaningful, and fitting a formula does not make it an exact law.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through four-representation dashboard, discrete versus continuous connection, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is inventing continuity or a unique formula from a small table.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Values 5,8,11,14 occur at x=0,1,2,3.

**Solution.** A linear candidate is f(x)=3x+5.

**Interpretation.** Constant first differences support the candidate.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Cost $25 plus $8 per person.

**Solution.** C(p)=25+8p.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses words, tables, graphs, and formulas in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Best form for exact f(37).

**Solution.** Formula.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Connecting data points claims that intermediate inputs are meaningful, and fitting a formula does not make it an exact law.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.4-V1 - Four-representation dashboard.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Values 5,8,11,14 occur at x=0,1,2,3. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: A linear candidate is f(x)=3x+5.

*Caption:* Four-representation dashboard: visual evidence for translate a function relationship among verbal, tabular, graphical, and algebraic representations while preserving quantities, units, and domain.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of four-representation dashboard, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.4-V2 - Discrete versus continuous connection.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Translate one representation at a time while preserving input, output, units, and domain; then verify agreement at shared values. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Discrete versus continuous connection: visual evidence for translate a function relationship among verbal, tabular, graphical, and algebraic representations while preserving quantities, units, and domain.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of discrete versus continuous connection, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.4-V3 - Exact rule versus fitted model.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is inventing continuity or a unique formula from a small table. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Exact rule versus fitted model: visual evidence for translate a function relationship among verbal, tabular, graphical, and algebraic representations while preserving quantities, units, and domain.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of exact rule versus fitted model, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

### Check yourself

<details><summary>Check</summary>

-1,1,3,5.

</details>

### Practice

1. Table f=2x+1 at -1,0,1,2.
2. Cost $25 plus $8 per person.
3. Best form for exact f(37).
4. Best form for turning points.
5. State the defining idea of words, tables, graphs, and formulas in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. -1,1,3,5.
2. C(p)=25+8p.
3. Formula.
4. Graph.
5. Words define quantities and assumptions, tables show selected pairs, graphs reveal global behavior, and formulas support exact calculation.
6. Connecting data points claims that intermediate inputs are meaningful, and fitting a formula does not make it an exact law.
7. A frequent error is inventing continuity or a unique formula from a small table.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P1.5. Domain from formulas

**Lesson outcome.** Determine the real domain of a function by combining denominator, even-root, logarithm, and composition restrictions.

### Opening

The real domain is the intersection of every input condition imposed by denominators, even roots, logarithms, and nested functions.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use domain from formulas to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Write each restriction separately, solve it, intersect the solution sets, and retain exclusions hidden by cancellation.

A square root in a denominator requires a strictly positive radicand, while an ordinary square root allows zero.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through restriction icons, condition intersection, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is checking only one restriction or using the simplified formula's larger domain.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Find domain of ln(x-1)/sqrt(x+2).

**Solution.** x>1.

**Interpretation.** The logarithm and denominator-root conditions are intersected.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Domain of sqrt(2-x).

**Solution.** x≤2.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses domain from formulas in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Domain of ln(x+3).

**Solution.** x>-3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A square root in a denominator requires a strictly positive radicand, while an ordinary square root allows zero.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.5-V1 - Restriction icons.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Find domain of ln(x-1)/sqrt(x+2). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: x>1.

*Caption:* Restriction icons: visual evidence for determine the real domain of a function by combining denominator, even-root, logarithm, and composition restrictions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of restriction icons, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.5-V2 - Condition intersection.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Write each restriction separately, solve it, intersect the solution sets, and retain exclusions hidden by cancellation. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Condition intersection: visual evidence for determine the real domain of a function by combining denominator, even-root, logarithm, and composition restrictions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of condition intersection, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.5-V3 - Retained hole.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is checking only one restriction or using the simplified formula's larger domain. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Retained hole: visual evidence for determine the real domain of a function by combining denominator, even-root, logarithm, and composition restrictions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of retained hole, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

### Check yourself

<details><summary>Check</summary>

x≠-4.

</details>

### Practice

2. Domain of sqrt(2-x).
3. Domain of ln(x+3).
4. Domain of sqrt(x^2-9).
5. State the defining idea of domain from formulas in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. x≠-4.
2. x≤2.
3. x>-3.
4. x≤-3 or x≥3.
5. The real domain is the intersection of every input condition imposed by denominators, even roots, logarithms, and nested functions.
6. A square root in a denominator requires a strictly positive radicand, while an ordinary square root allows zero.
7. A frequent error is checking only one restriction or using the simplified formula's larger domain.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.
10. The next lesson, Domain from context, uses this result as part of a larger structure.

## P1.6. Domain from context

**Lesson outcome.** Distinguish the algebraic domain of a formula from the inputs that are meaningful in a model.

### Opening

The algebraic domain states where a formula is defined; the contextual domain states where its inputs are meaningful for the model.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use domain from context to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Begin with the algebraic domain, then apply nonnegativity, capacity, integer, safety, time-window, and evidence-range restrictions.

Extrapolation outside observed inputs is an additional assumption even when the formula continues.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through nested domains, discrete versus continuous domains, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reporting all real numbers for a model involving time, length, population, or counts.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** A theater has capacity 240 and R(n)=12n.

**Solution.** n is an integer from 0 through 240.

**Interpretation.** Ticket count is discrete and capacity bounded.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Domain of 32-seat class size.

**Solution.** Integers 0 through 32.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses domain from context in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Classify household size.

**Solution.** Discrete.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Extrapolation outside observed inputs is an additional assumption even when the formula continues.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.6-V1 - Nested domains.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: A theater has capacity 240 and R(n)=12n. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: n is an integer from 0 through 240.

*Caption:* Nested domains: visual evidence for distinguish the algebraic domain of a formula from the inputs that are meaningful in a model.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of nested domains, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.6-V2 - Discrete versus continuous domains.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Begin with the algebraic domain, then apply nonnegativity, capacity, integer, safety, time-window, and evidence-range restrictions. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Discrete versus continuous domains: visual evidence for distinguish the algebraic domain of a formula from the inputs that are meaningful in a model.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of discrete versus continuous domains, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.6-V3 - Extrapolation warning.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is reporting all real numbers for a model involving time, length, population, or counts. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Extrapolation warning: visual evidence for distinguish the algebraic domain of a formula from the inputs that are meaningful in a model.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of extrapolation warning, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

### Check yourself

<details><summary>Check</summary>

</details>

### Practice

2. Domain of 32-seat class size.
3. Classify household size.
4. Define interpolation.
5. State the defining idea of domain from context in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. s≥0.
2. Integers 0 through 32.
3. Discrete.
4. Prediction within observed inputs.
5. The algebraic domain states where a formula is defined; the contextual domain states where its inputs are meaningful for the model.
6. Extrapolation outside observed inputs is an additional assumption even when the formula continues.
7. A frequent error is reporting all real numbers for a model involving time, length, population, or counts.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.
10. The next lesson, Range and global graph behavior, uses this result as part of a larger structure.

## P1.7. Range and global graph behavior

**Lesson outcome.** Read range, intercepts, extrema, boundedness, symmetry, and intervals of increase or decrease from a graph.

### Opening

A graph reveals range, intercepts, extrema, boundedness, symmetry, and intervals of change.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use range and global graph behavior to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Project onto the axes for domain and range, label intercepts as points, and report increase or decrease intervals with input values.

Open endpoints, arrows, asymptotes, and missing points affect whether boundary values belong to the range.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through domain-range projections, behavior intervals, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is writing outputs as intervals of increase or calling every local peak an absolute maximum.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** A graph has included minimum -3 and no upper bound.

**Solution.** Range [-3,∞).

**Interpretation.** The bracket records the attained minimum.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Maximum y-intercepts for a function.

**Solution.** One.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses range and global graph behavior in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Point for f(2)=0.

**Solution.** (2,0).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Open endpoints, arrows, asymptotes, and missing points affect whether boundary values belong to the range.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.7-V1 - Domain-range projections.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: A graph has included minimum -3 and no upper bound. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Range [-3,∞).

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of domain-range projections, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.7-V2 - Behavior intervals.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Project onto the axes for domain and range, label intercepts as points, and report increase or decrease intervals with input values. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Behavior intervals: visual evidence for read range, intercepts, extrema, boundedness, symmetry, and intervals of increase or decrease from a graph.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of behavior intervals, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of local versus absolute extrema, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. One.

## P1.8. Piecewise-defined functions

**Lesson outcome.** Evaluate, graph, and interpret functions that use different rules on different parts of the domain.

### Opening

A piecewise function uses different rules on nonoverlapping parts of one domain.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use piecewise-defined functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Locate the input interval first, evaluate with that rule, and graph each restricted piece with correct open or closed endpoints.

At a boundary, continuity requires both one-sided behaviors and the defined value to agree.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through piecewise rule selector, endpoint gallery, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is evaluating every formula or including both boundary points.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** f=x+2 for x<1 and x^2 for x≥1; find f(-3),f(1),f(2).

**Solution.** -1,1,4.

**Interpretation.** The boundary belongs to the second rule.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Meaning of overlapping intervals.

**Solution.** The definition may assign two outputs.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses piecewise-defined functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Meaning of omitted boundary.

**Solution.** The function is undefined there.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: At a boundary, continuity requires both one-sided behaviors and the defined value to agree.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.8-V1 - Piecewise rule selector.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: f=x+2 for x<1 and x^2 for x≥1; find f(-3),f(1),f(2). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: -1,1,4.

*Caption:* Piecewise rule selector: visual evidence for evaluate, graph, and interpret functions that use different rules on different parts of the domain.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of piecewise rule selector, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.8-V2 - Endpoint gallery.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Locate the input interval first, evaluate with that rule, and graph each restricted piece with correct open or closed endpoints. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Endpoint gallery: visual evidence for evaluate, graph, and interpret functions that use different rules on different parts of the domain.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of endpoint gallery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.8-V3 - Boundary behavior comparison.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is evaluating every formula or including both boundary points. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Boundary behavior comparison: visual evidence for evaluate, graph, and interpret functions that use different rules on different parts of the domain.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of boundary behavior comparison, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

3. The function is undefined there.

6. At a boundary, continuity requires both one-sided behaviors and the defined value to agree.
7. A frequent error is evaluating every formula or including both boundary points.
