## P1.9. Average rate of change

**Lesson outcome.** Calculate and interpret average rate of change as output change per input change and as the slope of a secant line.

### Opening

Average rate of change from a to b is [f(b)-f(a)]/(b-a), the slope of the secant line through the endpoint values.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use average rate of change to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Evaluate both endpoints, subtract in matching order, simplify, attach compound units, and interpret the sign over the interval.

The inputs must be distinct, and the rate summarizes the interval rather than every interior moment.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through movable secant, same average, different paths, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reversing only one difference or omitting units.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Find average rate of x^2 from 1 to 4.

**Solution.** 5.

**Interpretation.** The secant rises 15 over a run of 3.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Average rate of x^2-3x from 2 to 5.

**Solution.** 4.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses average rate of change in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Why a≠b?

**Solution.** The denominator cannot be zero.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The inputs must be distinct, and the rate summarizes the interval rather than every interior moment.

### Figure storyboard

**P1.9-V1 - Movable secant.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Find average rate of x^2 from 1 to 4. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: 5.

*Caption:* Movable secant: visual evidence for calculate and interpret average rate of change as output change per input change and as the slope of a secant line.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of movable secant, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.9-V2 - Same average, different paths.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Evaluate both endpoints, subtract in matching order, simplify, attach compound units, and interpret the sign over the interval. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Same average, different paths: visual evidence for calculate and interpret average rate of change as output change per input change and as the slope of a secant line.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of same average, different paths, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P1.9-V3 - Interval-dependent secants.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is reversing only one difference or omitting units. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Interval-dependent secants: visual evidence for calculate and interpret average rate of change as output change per input change and as the slope of a secant line.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of interval-dependent secants, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

2.

</details>

2. Average rate of x^2-3x from 2 to 5.

6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

## P1.10. Function representation and modeling studio

**Lesson outcome.** Construct, compare, and defend function representations while stating assumptions, domain, units, and limitations.

### Opening

A model is a deliberately simplified function used to answer a question; it is not the real system itself.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use function representation and modeling studio to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use function notation from P0.
2. Read ordered pairs and interval notation.
3. Attach units to contextual quantities.

### Exact learner exposition

Define quantities, choose a family, estimate parameters, state domain and assumptions, inspect residuals, and revise or reject when evidence disagrees.

Interpolation and extrapolation must be distinguished, and causation requires evidence beyond correlation.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through modeling cycle, competing extrapolations, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is choosing the smoothest graph or highest fit statistic without checking meaning.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Costs 65,90,115,140 for 1-4 hours.

**Solution.** C(h)=40+25h.

**Interpretation.** The fixed fee and hourly rate are interpretable.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Why inspect residual patterns?

**Solution.** They reveal systematic model failure.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses function representation and modeling studio in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Give interpolation example.

**Solution.** Estimate within observed range.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Interpolation and extrapolation must be distinguished, and causation requires evidence beyond correlation.

The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of competing extrapolations, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## Unit investigation

The investigation for Functions and Multiple Representations combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

## Unit review and mastery

The review mixes current-unit questions with retrieval from earlier units. The mastery assessment balances symbolic fluency, graph and table interpretation, explanation, error analysis, and modeling transfer.


\pagebreak

# Unit P2: Transformations and Function Operations

**Governing question.** How can a familiar function be moved, reflected, scaled, and combined while retaining recognizable structure?

This unit treats familiar functions as reference objects that can be transformed and combined through coordinate mappings.

## Unit lessons

## P2.1. Parent-function atlas

**Lesson outcome.** Recognize benchmark function families by domain, range, symmetry, intercepts, end behavior, and rate patterns.

### Opening

A parent function is a convenient reference member identified by domain, range, symmetry, endpoints, asymptotes, and rate patterns.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use parent-function atlas to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Compare benchmark families on common axes and use numerical differences or ratios as additional evidence.

A graph window can hide asymptotes or end behavior, so classification should use more than silhouette.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through parent-function gallery, feature table, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is naming a family from appearance without checking restrictions.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Defined for x≥0, starts at the origin, increases while flattening.

**Solution.** Square-root family.

**Interpretation.** The endpoint and domain are decisive.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Parent with sharp corner.

**Solution.** Absolute value.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses parent-function atlas in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Quadratic table pattern.

**Solution.** Constant second differences.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A graph window can hide asymptotes or end behavior, so classification should use more than silhouette.

**P2.1-V2 - Feature table.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Compare benchmark families on common axes and use numerical differences or ratios as additional evidence. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

## P2.2. Coordinate mappings and transformation logic

**Lesson outcome.** Derive graph transformations by tracking how input-output pairs move.

### Opening

A graph transformation is a coordinate map from a point (u,v) on the parent to a point on the transformed graph.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use coordinate mappings and transformation logic to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Outside operations alter v; inside operations determine the new x by solving the inside expression equal to u.

For a f(b(x-h))+k, the map is (u,v) to (h+u/b,av+k).

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through input-output machine, coordinate mapping table, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is applying an inside parameter directly instead of using its reciprocal on x-coordinates.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Map (8,3) under f(4x).

**Solution.** (2,3).

**Interpretation.** Horizontal coordinates divide by 4.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Map under f(x-2).

**Solution.** (3,4).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses coordinate mappings and transformation logic in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Map under -f(x).

**Solution.** (1,-4).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: For a f(b(x-h))+k, the map is (u,v) to (h+u/b,av+k).

### Figure storyboard

**P2.2-V1 - Input-output machine.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Map (8,3) under f(4x). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: (2,3).

*Caption:* Input-output machine: visual evidence for derive graph transformations by tracking how input-output pairs move.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of input-output machine, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.2-V2 - Coordinate mapping table.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Outside operations alter v; inside operations determine the new x by solving the inside expression equal to u. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Coordinate mapping table: visual evidence for derive graph transformations by tracking how input-output pairs move.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of coordinate mapping table, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.2-V3 - Horizontal sign-reversal derivation.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is applying an inside parameter directly instead of using its reciprocal on x-coordinates. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Horizontal sign-reversal derivation: visual evidence for derive graph transformations by tracking how input-output pairs move.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of horizontal sign-reversal derivation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

</details>

10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. (3,4).
3. (1,-4).

6. For a f(b(x-h))+k, the map is (u,v) to (h+u/b,av+k).
7. A frequent error is applying an inside parameter directly instead of using its reciprocal on x-coordinates.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P2.3. Vertical translations

**Lesson outcome.** Analyze how adding a constant outside a function changes outputs, range, intercepts, extrema, and asymptotes.

### Opening

Adding k outside a function changes every output by k and moves the graph vertically.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use vertical translations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Map (a,b) to (a,b+k), translate the range and horizontal asymptote, and recompute zeros.

The domain and input locations of extrema remain unchanged.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through vertical point mapping, feature ledger, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is moving x-coordinates or assuming x-intercepts simply shift vertically.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Describe x^2-5.

**Solution.** Vertex (0,-5), range [-5,∞), zeros ±sqrt(5).

**Interpretation.** The parabola shifts down 5.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Range of sqrt(x)-2.

**Solution.** [-2,∞).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses vertical translations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** HA of 5^x+7.

**Solution.** y=7.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The domain and input locations of extrema remain unchanged.

### Figure storyboard

**P2.3-V1 - Vertical point mapping.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Describe x^2-5. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Vertex (0,-5), range [-5,∞), zeros ±sqrt(5).

*Caption:* Vertical point mapping: visual evidence for analyze how adding a constant outside a function changes outputs, range, intercepts, extrema, and asymptotes.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of vertical point mapping, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.3-V2 - Feature ledger.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Map (a,b) to (a,b+k), translate the range and horizontal asymptote, and recompute zeros. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Feature ledger: visual evidence for analyze how adding a constant outside a function changes outputs, range, intercepts, extrema, and asymptotes.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of feature ledger, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.3-V3 - Baseline interpretation.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is moving x-coordinates or assuming x-intercepts simply shift vertically. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Baseline interpretation: visual evidence for analyze how adding a constant outside a function changes outputs, range, intercepts, extrema, and asymptotes.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of baseline interpretation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P2.4. Horizontal translations

**Lesson outcome.** Analyze how replacing x by x-h changes input locations, domain, intercepts, extrema, and vertical asymptotes.

### Opening

Replacing x by x-h relocates every input feature h units to the right.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use horizontal translations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Solve the inside equation for the new x-coordinate; translate domain endpoints, zeros, extrema, and vertical asymptotes.

The range remains unchanged because the same outputs occur at new inputs.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through input relocation, domain interval translation, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reading the inside sign literally without solving for the old input.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Describe sqrt(x-4).

**Solution.** Endpoint (4,0), domain [4,∞).

**Interpretation.** The root graph shifts right 4.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Vertex of (x-7)^2.

**Solution.** (7,0).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses horizontal translations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Move (-2,4] right 3.

**Solution.** (1,7].

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The range remains unchanged because the same outputs occur at new inputs.

### Figure storyboard

**P2.4-V1 - Input relocation.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Describe sqrt(x-4). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Endpoint (4,0), domain [4,∞).

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of input relocation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.4-V2 - Domain interval translation.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Solve the inside equation for the new x-coordinate; translate domain endpoints, zeros, extrema, and vertical asymptotes. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Domain interval translation: visual evidence for analyze how replacing x by x-h changes input locations, domain, intercepts, extrema, and vertical asymptotes.

## P2.5. Reflections and symmetry

**Lesson outcome.** Reflect function graphs across coordinate axes and connect reflection rules with even and odd symmetry.

### Opening

The graph of -f(x) reflects across the x-axis; f(-x) reflects across the y-axis.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use reflections and symmetry to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Negate output coordinates or input coordinates, then test f(-x)=f(x) for even symmetry and f(-x)=-f(x) for odd symmetry.

Symmetry claims require a domain symmetric about zero.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through axis reflection mappings, even-odd-neither gallery, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is confusing -f(x) with f(-x).

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Classify x^4-3x^2+1.

**Solution.** Even.

**Interpretation.** Substituting -x leaves the formula unchanged.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Reflect sqrt(x) across y-axis.

**Solution.** sqrt(-x).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses reflections and symmetry in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Map (3,-5) across x-axis.

**Solution.** (3,5).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Symmetry claims require a domain symmetric about zero.

### Figure storyboard

*Caption:* Axis reflection mappings: visual evidence for reflect function graphs across coordinate axes and connect reflection rules with even and odd symmetry.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of axis reflection mappings, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.5-V2 - Even-odd-neither gallery.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Negate output coordinates or input coordinates, then test f(-x)=f(x) for even symmetry and f(-x)=-f(x) for odd symmetry. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Even-odd-neither gallery: visual evidence for reflect function graphs across coordinate axes and connect reflection rules with even and odd symmetry.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of even-odd-neither gallery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.5-V3 - Symmetry table.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is confusing -f(x) with f(-x). Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

## P2.6. Vertical and horizontal scaling

**Lesson outcome.** Distinguish output scaling from input scaling and determine the resulting stretches, compressions, domains, and ranges.

### Opening

Multiplying outputs scales vertically; replacing x by cx divides horizontal coordinates by c.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use vertical and horizontal scaling to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Use (a,b) to (a,cb) for cf(x) and (a/c,b) for f(cx).

Negative scale factors also reflect, and domain boundaries follow the x-coordinate map.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through scaling point grid, parabola scale comparison, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is multiplying horizontal coordinates by c instead of dividing.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Map (6,4) under -2f(3x).

**Solution.** (2,-8).

**Interpretation.** Inputs divide by 3; outputs multiply by -2.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Describe f(x/3).

**Solution.** Horizontal stretch 3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses vertical and horizontal scaling in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Map (8,-1) under f(4x).

**Solution.** (2,-1).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Negative scale factors also reflect, and domain boundaries follow the x-coordinate map.

### Figure storyboard

**P2.6-V1 - Scaling point grid.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Map (6,4) under -2f(3x). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: (2,-8).

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of scaling point grid, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.6-V2 - Parabola scale comparison.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Use (a,b) to (a,cb) for cf(x) and (a/c,b) for f(cx). The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Parabola scale comparison: visual evidence for distinguish output scaling from input scaling and determine the resulting stretches, compressions, domains, and ranges.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of parabola scale comparison, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.6-V3 - Boundary scaling.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is multiplying horizontal coordinates by c instead of dividing. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Boundary scaling: visual evidence for distinguish output scaling from input scaling and determine the resulting stretches, compressions, domains, and ranges.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of boundary scaling, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

Vertical stretch 5.

</details>

### Practice

2. Describe f(x/3).
3. Map (8,-1) under f(4x).
4. Range [1,4] under -2f.
5. State the defining idea of vertical and horizontal scaling in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. Vertical stretch 5.
2. Horizontal stretch 3.
3. (2,-1).
4. [-8,-2].
5. Multiplying outputs scales vertically; replacing x by cx divides horizontal coordinates by c.
6. Negative scale factors also reflect, and domain boundaries follow the x-coordinate map.
7. A frequent error is multiplying horizontal coordinates by c instead of dividing.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P2.7. Combined transformations

**Lesson outcome.** Analyze and graph functions with several transformations by tracking inputs, outputs, and key features.

### Opening

Combined transformations are governed by one coordinate map rather than a memorized drawing order.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use combined transformations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

For a f(b(x-h))+k, map (u,v) to (h+u/b,av+k), beginning with vertices, endpoints, zeros, or asymptotes.

Equivalent algebraic forms may describe the same final map through different verbal sequences.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through general coordinate map, feature-first builder, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is plotting many points before mapping the defining features.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Graph -2|x-3|+5.

**Solution.** Vertex (3,5), range (-∞,5], reflected and stretched.

**Interpretation.** The vertex and symmetric points anchor the graph.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Endpoint of -sqrt(x-5)+4.

**Solution.** (5,4).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses combined transformations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Asymptotes of 2/(x+1)-3.

**Solution.** x=-1,y=-3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Equivalent algebraic forms may describe the same final map through different verbal sequences.

### Figure storyboard

**P2.7-V1 - General coordinate map.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Graph -2|x-3|+5. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Vertex (3,5), range (-∞,5], reflected and stretched.

*Caption:* General coordinate map: visual evidence for analyze and graph functions with several transformations by tracking inputs, outputs, and key features.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of general coordinate map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.7-V2 - Feature-first builder.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: For a f(b(x-h))+k, map (u,v) to (h+u/b,av+k), beginning with vertices, endpoints, zeros, or asymptotes. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Feature-first builder: visual evidence for analyze and graph functions with several transformations by tracking inputs, outputs, and key features.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of feature-first builder, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.7-V3 - Equivalent sequences.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is plotting many points before mapping the defining features. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of equivalent sequences, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.

## P2.8. Sums and differences of functions

**Lesson outcome.** Add and subtract functions pointwise, determine the common domain, and interpret combined outputs.

### Opening

Function sums and differences combine outputs at the same input.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use sums and differences of functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Compute f(x) and g(x), add or subtract them pointwise, and intersect the original domains.

Units must be compatible, and subtraction order matters.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through pointwise addition, domain intersection, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is confusing f(x)+g(x) with composition f(x+g(x)).

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Revenue 30q and cost 500+18q.

**Solution.** Profit 12q-500.

**Interpretation.** A difference function represents net value.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** If f(2)=5,g(2)=-1.

**Solution.** 4.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses sums and differences of functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Domain sqrt(x-3)+ln x.

**Solution.** [3,∞).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Units must be compatible, and subtraction order matters.

### Figure storyboard

**P2.8-V1 - Pointwise addition.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Revenue 30q and cost 500+18q. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Profit 12q-500.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of pointwise addition, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.8-V2 - Domain intersection.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Compute f(x) and g(x), add or subtract them pointwise, and intersect the original domains. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Domain intersection: visual evidence for add and subtract functions pointwise, determine the common domain, and interpret combined outputs.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of domain intersection, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.8-V3 - Revenue-cost-profit gap.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is confusing f(x)+g(x) with composition f(x+g(x)). Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of revenue-cost-profit gap, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

</details>

2. If f(2)=5,g(2)=-1.
3. Domain sqrt(x-3)+ln x.

5. State the defining idea of sums and differences of functions in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

## P2.9. Products and quotients of functions

**Lesson outcome.** Multiply and divide functions pointwise while analyzing zeros, restrictions, and combined graph behavior.

### Opening

Product functions multiply outputs; quotient functions divide outputs and exclude every denominator-function zero.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use products and quotients of functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Intersect domains, factor, locate product zeros, add quotient restrictions, and use signs to analyze the graph.

A cancelled denominator factor still excludes its original input.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through pointwise multiplication, zero-set union, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is forgetting the extra quotient condition g(x)≠0.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** f=x^2-1,g=x-1.

**Solution.** f/g=x+1 for x≠1, with a hole at (1,2).

**Interpretation.** Cancellation preserves the excluded input.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Domain (x+1)/x^2.

**Solution.** x≠0.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses products and quotients of functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** If f(3)=2,g(3)=-4, product.

**Solution.** -8.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A cancelled denominator factor still excludes its original input.

### Figure storyboard

**P2.9-V1 - Pointwise multiplication.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: f=x^2-1,g=x-1. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: f/g=x+1 for x≠1, with a hole at (1,2).

*Caption:* Pointwise multiplication: visual evidence for multiply and divide functions pointwise while analyzing zeros, restrictions, and combined graph behavior.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of pointwise multiplication, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.9-V2 - Zero-set union.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Intersect domains, factor, locate product zeros, add quotient restrictions, and use signs to analyze the graph. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Zero-set union: visual evidence for multiply and divide functions pointwise while analyzing zeros, restrictions, and combined graph behavior.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of zero-set union, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.9-V3 - Hole-asymptote preview.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is forgetting the extra quotient condition g(x)≠0. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Hole-asymptote preview: visual evidence for multiply and divide functions pointwise while analyzing zeros, restrictions, and combined graph behavior.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of hole-asymptote preview, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

</details>

### Practice

2. Domain (x+1)/x^2.

4. Quotient.

6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. x≠0.
3. -8.
4. -1/2.

6. A cancelled denominator factor still excludes its original input.
7. A frequent error is forgetting the extra quotient condition g(x)≠0.

## P2.10. Reconstructing formulas from transformed graphs

**Lesson outcome.** Use parent-family features, transformations, and one or more data points to construct and verify a function formula.

### Opening

Visible graph features determine a parameterized family form; one additional point often determines the remaining scale.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use reconstructing formulas from transformed graphs to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Recognize the parent function family.
2. Read domain, range, and key points.
3. Use coordinate mappings.

### Exact learner exposition

Choose a family from vertex, endpoint, or asymptote structure, write a feature-based form, solve its scale parameter, and verify.

A finite point set does not determine a unique family without structural evidence.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through feature-to-form map, parameter recovery, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is guessing from appearance and never checking a second point or domain.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Parabola vertex (2,-3), point (4,5).

**Solution.** f(x)=2(x-2)^2-3.

**Interpretation.** The vertex fixes h,k and the point fixes a.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Absolute value vertex (4,-1), point (6,5).

**Solution.** 3|x-4|-1.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses reconstructing formulas from transformed graphs in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Root endpoint (2,-3), point (6,1).

**Solution.** 2sqrt(x-2)-3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A finite point set does not determine a unique family without structural evidence.

### Figure storyboard

**P2.10-V1 - Feature-to-form map.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Parabola vertex (2,-3), point (4,5). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: f(x)=2(x-2)^2-3.

*Caption:* Feature-to-form map: visual evidence for use parent-family features, transformations, and one or more data points to construct and verify a function formula.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of feature-to-form map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.10-V2 - Parameter recovery.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Choose a family from vertex, endpoint, or asymptote structure, write a feature-based form, solve its scale parameter, and verify. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Parameter recovery: visual evidence for use parent-family features, transformations, and one or more data points to construct and verify a function formula.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of parameter recovery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P2.10-V3 - Competing formulas.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is guessing from appearance and never checking a second point or domain. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

## Unit investigation

The investigation for Transformations and Function Operations combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

## Unit review and mastery

The review mixes current-unit questions with retrieval from earlier units. The mastery assessment balances symbolic fluency, graph and table interpretation, explanation, error analysis, and modeling transfer.


\pagebreak

# Unit P3: Composition, Inverses, and Modeling

**Governing question.** How do processes combine, and under what conditions can a process be reversed?

This unit treats functions as ordered processes that can sometimes be reversed.

## Unit lessons

## P3.1. Composition as sequential processing

**Lesson outcome.** Interpret f composed with g as applying g first and then f, and explain why order generally matters.

### Opening

Composition represents an ordered chain in which the inner function acts first.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use composition as sequential processing to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Trace the original input through the first function, name the intermediate quantity, and feed it to the second function.

The intermediate output must lie in the outer function's domain and have compatible units.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through two-stage machine, order comparison, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reading f composed with g from left to right as though f acts first.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Coupon d(p)=p-20 and tax t(q)=1.06q at p=100.

**Solution.** t(d(100))=84.80, while d(t(100))=86.

**Interpretation.** A fixed coupon and percentage tax do not commute.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** g(f(3)).

**Solution.** 26.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses composition as sequential processing in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Which acts first in f(g(x))?

**Solution.** g.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The intermediate output must lie in the outer function's domain and have compatible units.

### Figure storyboard

## P3.2. Composition from formulas

**Lesson outcome.** Evaluate and simplify composite functions symbolically while preserving grouping and domain conditions.

### Opening

Formula composition replaces every occurrence of the outer variable with the complete inner expression.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use composition from formulas to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Write the substitution with parentheses before simplifying and determine both inner and outer domain conditions.

Simplification may hide restrictions inherited from an intermediate stage.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through substitution slots, expression trees, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is substituting into only one term or dropping grouping symbols.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** f=x^2-1,g=3x+2.

**Solution.** f(g(x))=9x^2+12x+3.

**Interpretation.** The entire linear expression is squared.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** g after f.

**Solution.** (2x+3)^2.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses composition from formulas in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** f=1/x,g=x-4; domain.

**Solution.** x≠4.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Simplification may hide restrictions inherited from an intermediate stage.

*Caption:* Outer restriction preimage: visual evidence for evaluate and simplify composite functions symbolically while preserving grouping and domain conditions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of outer restriction preimage, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P3.3. Composition from tables and graphs

**Lesson outcome.** Evaluate composite functions when one or both functions are represented numerically or graphically.

### Opening

Composition from tables or graphs uses two successive input-output readings.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use composition from tables and graphs to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Find the inner output, then use it as the outer input, preserving approximation when a graph is read visually.

A missing table entry is not automatically an excluded domain value; distinguish insufficient data from undefinedness.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through table lookup chain, graph-to-graph trace, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is using the original input in both functions.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** g(2)=4 and f(4)=7.

**Solution.** f(g(2))=7.

**Interpretation.** The intermediate value becomes the next input.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** f(-2)=5,g(5)=0.

**Solution.** 0.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses composition from tables and graphs in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Undefined versus unknown.

**Solution.** Excluded by domain versus missing data.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A missing table entry is not automatically an excluded domain value; distinguish insufficient data from undefinedness.

## P3.4. Domains of composite functions

**Lesson outcome.** Determine the domain of a composite by enforcing both inner-function and outer-input conditions.

### Opening

The domain of f(g(x)) requires x to be allowed by g and g(x) to be allowed by f.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use domains of composite functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Translate outer restrictions into equations or inequalities involving the inner output, then intersect them with the inner domain.

One forbidden outer input can have several original preimages.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through composite-domain pipeline, preimage of forbidden value, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is simply intersecting the written domains of f and g as though they used the same stage variable.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** f=sqrt(u),g=x^2-9.

**Solution.** Domain x≤-3 or x≥3.

**Interpretation.** The outer root condition becomes x^2-9≥0.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Domain 1/(x^2-16).

**Solution.** x≠±4.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses domains of composite functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Domain ln(4-x^2).

**Solution.** (-2,2).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: One forbidden outer input can have several original preimages.

### Figure storyboard

**P3.4-V1 - Composite-domain pipeline.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: f=sqrt(u),g=x^2-9. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Domain x≤-3 or x≥3.

*Caption:* Composite-domain pipeline: visual evidence for determine the domain of a composite by enforcing both inner-function and outer-input conditions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of composite-domain pipeline, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.4-V2 - Preimage of forbidden value.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Translate outer restrictions into equations or inequalities involving the inner output, then intersect them with the inner domain. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Preimage of forbidden value: visual evidence for determine the domain of a composite by enforcing both inner-function and outer-input conditions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of preimage of forbidden value, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.4-V3 - Hidden restriction.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is simply intersecting the written domains of f and g as though they used the same stage variable. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Hidden restriction: visual evidence for determine the domain of a composite by enforcing both inner-function and outer-input conditions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of hidden restriction, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

</details>

10. State how this lesson connects forward.

## P3.5. One-to-one functions

**Lesson outcome.** Determine whether a function is one-to-one and explain why one-to-one behavior is required for an inverse function.

### Opening

A one-to-one function gives each output at most one input, making reverse assignment possible.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use one-to-one functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Check repeated outputs, use the horizontal-line test, or prove f(a)=f(b) implies a=b.

A function that fails globally may be restricted to a monotonic interval.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through horizontal-line test, reverse mapping failure, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is confusing the ordinary function condition with one-to-one behavior.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Is x^2 one-to-one on all real numbers?

**Solution.** No; x and -x share outputs. Restrict to x≥0 or x≤0.

**Interpretation.** A branch restriction restores reverse uniqueness.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Is x^3 one-to-one?

**Solution.** Yes.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses one-to-one functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Horizontal-line test.

**Solution.** Every horizontal line meets at most once.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A function that fails globally may be restricted to a monotonic interval.

### Figure storyboard

**P3.5-V1 - Horizontal-line test.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Is x^2 one-to-one on all real numbers? Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: No; x and -x share outputs. Restrict to x≥0 or x≤0.

*Caption:* Horizontal-line test: visual evidence for determine whether a function is one-to-one and explain why one-to-one behavior is required for an inverse function.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of horizontal-line test, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.5-V2 - Reverse mapping failure.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Check repeated outputs, use the horizontal-line test, or prove f(a)=f(b) implies a=b. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Reverse mapping failure: visual evidence for determine whether a function is one-to-one and explain why one-to-one behavior is required for an inverse function.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of reverse mapping failure, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.5-V3 - Quadratic branch restrictions.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is confusing the ordinary function condition with one-to-one behavior. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Quadratic branch restrictions: visual evidence for determine whether a function is one-to-one and explain why one-to-one behavior is required for an inverse function.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of quadratic branch restrictions, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P3.6. Constructing inverse functions

**Lesson outcome.** Construct an inverse function algebraically and state the swapped domain and range.

### Opening

An inverse function reverses input and output roles; it is not the reciprocal 1/f.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use constructing inverse functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Write y=f(x), exchange x and y, solve for y, and state the swapped domain and range.

The original function must be one-to-one on its stated domain, and a restricted branch determines any root sign.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through coordinate reversal, algebraic inversion, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is keeping both plus and minus branches and producing an inverse relation rather than a function.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Find inverse of 3x-7.

**Solution.** f^{-1}(x)=(x+7)/3.

**Interpretation.** The inverse reverses the operations in reverse order.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Inverse of 4x.

**Solution.** x/4.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses constructing inverse functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Inverse of (x+3)/2.

**Solution.** 2x-3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The original function must be one-to-one on its stated domain, and a restricted branch determines any root sign.

### Figure storyboard

**P3.6-V1 - Coordinate reversal.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Find inverse of 3x-7. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: f^{-1}(x)=(x+7)/3.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of coordinate reversal, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.6-V2 - Algebraic inversion.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Write y=f(x), exchange x and y, solve for y, and state the swapped domain and range. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Algebraic inversion: visual evidence for construct an inverse function algebraically and state the swapped domain and range.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of algebraic inversion, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.6-V3 - Inverse versus reciprocal.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is keeping both plus and minus branches and producing an inverse relation rather than a function. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Inverse versus reciprocal: visual evidence for construct an inverse function algebraically and state the swapped domain and range.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of inverse versus reciprocal, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P3.7. Graphs and verification of inverses

**Lesson outcome.** Verify inverse functions using composition and graph symmetry across y=x.

### Opening

Inverse functions undo each other in both composition orders and reflect across y=x.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use graphs and verification of inverses to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Compute f(f^{-1}(x)) and f^{-1}(f(x)), preserving their valid domains, and compare graph point pairs.

The two compositions begin on different domains: the original range and original domain.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through inverse reflection, composition loop, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is simplifying sqrt(x^2) to x without the branch restriction.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Verify f=3x-2 and g=(x+2)/3.

**Solution.** Both compositions simplify to x.

**Interpretation.** The functions undo each other.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Reflect (3,-1) across y=x.

**Solution.** (-1,3).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses graphs and verification of inverses in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Inverse range if original domain [2,∞).

**Solution.** [2,∞).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The two compositions begin on different domains: the original range and original domain.

### Figure storyboard

## P3.8. Domain restrictions and radical inverses

**Lesson outcome.** Restrict power functions to one-to-one branches and connect those branches to principal radical functions.

### Opening

Even roots require nonnegative radicands and the principal square root is nonnegative, so sqrt(x^2)=|x|.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use domain restrictions and radical inverses to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Extract perfect powers, convert between radical and rational-exponent notation, isolate a radical in an equation, raise both sides to a power, and check every candidate.

Negative rational exponents also require a nonzero base. Squaring is not reversible without sign and domain checks.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through principal root versus equation roots, radical-exponent equivalence, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is replacing sqrt(x^2) with x for negative inputs or omitting the final check.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve sqrt(x+6)=x.

**Solution.** Candidates are 3 and -2; only x=3 satisfies the original equation.

**Interpretation.** The domain removes the extraneous value.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Evaluate 16^(3/4).

**Solution.** 8.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses domain restrictions and radical inverses in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** State domain of sqrt(3-x).

**Solution.** x≤3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Negative rational exponents also require a nonzero base. Squaring is not reversible without sign and domain checks.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of principal root versus equation roots, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.8-V2 - Radical-exponent equivalence.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Extract perfect powers, convert between radical and rational-exponent notation, isolate a radical in an equation, raise both sides to a power, and check every candidate. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Radical-exponent equivalence: visual evidence for restrict power functions to one-to-one branches and connect those branches to principal radical functions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of radical-exponent equivalence, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.8-V3 - Extraneous-solution map.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is replacing sqrt(x^2) with x for negative inputs or omitting the final check. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of extraneous-solution map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P3.9. Model construction and validation

**Lesson outcome.** Build a multi-stage function model, state its domain and assumptions, and evaluate whether an inverse interpretation is meaningful.

### Opening

A model is a deliberately simplified function used to answer a question; it is not the real system itself.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use model construction and validation to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Evaluate function notation.
2. Determine domains from formulas.
3. Solve equations for a selected variable.

### Exact learner exposition

Define quantities, choose a family, estimate parameters, state domain and assumptions, inspect residuals, and revise or reject when evidence disagrees.

Interpolation and extrapolation must be distinguished, and causation requires evidence beyond correlation.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through modeling cycle, competing extrapolations, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is choosing the smoothest graph or highest fit statistic without checking meaning.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Costs 65,90,115,140 for 1-4 hours.

**Solution.** C(h)=40+25h.

**Interpretation.** The fixed fee and hourly rate are interpretable.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Why inspect residual patterns?

**Solution.** They reveal systematic model failure.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses model construction and validation in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Give interpolation example.

**Solution.** Estimate within observed range.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Interpolation and extrapolation must be distinguished, and causation requires evidence beyond correlation.

### Figure storyboard

**P3.9-V1 - Modeling cycle.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Costs 65,90,115,140 for 1-4 hours. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: C(h)=40+25h.

*Caption:* Modeling cycle: visual evidence for build a multi-stage function model, state its domain and assumptions, and evaluate whether an inverse interpretation is meaningful.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of modeling cycle, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.9-V2 - Competing extrapolations.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Define quantities, choose a family, estimate parameters, state domain and assumptions, inspect residuals, and revise or reject when evidence disagrees. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Competing extrapolations: visual evidence for build a multi-stage function model, state its domain and assumptions, and evaluate whether an inverse interpretation is meaningful.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of competing extrapolations, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P3.9-V3 - Residual plot.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is choosing the smoothest graph or highest fit statistic without checking meaning. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Residual plot: visual evidence for build a multi-stage function model, state its domain and assumptions, and evaluate whether an inverse interpretation is meaningful.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of residual plot, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

</details>

10. State how this lesson connects forward.

## Unit investigation

The investigation for Composition, Inverses, and Modeling combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

## Unit review and mastery

The review mixes current-unit questions with retrieval from earlier units. The mastery assessment balances symbolic fluency, graph and table interpretation, explanation, error analysis, and modeling transfer.


\pagebreak

# Unit P4: Polynomial Functions

**Governing question.** How do degree, leading behavior, zeros, and multiplicity determine a polynomial's global shape?

This unit studies how polynomial algebra determines global graph behavior.

## Unit lessons

## P4.1. Power functions and dominant behavior

**Lesson outcome.** Compare power functions f(x)=ax^p and explain how exponent and coefficient control domain, symmetry, end behavior, and growth.

### Opening

Power functions f(x)=ax^p connect positive, negative, and fractional exponent behavior.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use power functions and dominant behavior to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Classify the exponent, state domain and symmetry, determine end behavior, and compare growth over a stated input region.

Higher powers dominate for large magnitude but may be smaller between zero and one.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through power-family grid, large-input comparison, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is describing every power only as steeper or flatter.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Describe -2x^4.

**Solution.** Even, all-real domain, both ends down, range (-∞,0].

**Interpretation.** Parity and sign control the broad shape.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Domain of x^-3.

**Solution.** x≠0.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses power functions and dominant behavior in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Range of 1/x^2.

**Solution.** (0,∞).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Higher powers dominate for large magnitude but may be smaller between zero and one.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of power-family grid, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P4.1-V2 - Large-input comparison.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Classify the exponent, state domain and symmetry, determine end behavior, and compare growth over a stated input region. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Large-input comparison: visual evidence for compare power functions f(x)=ax^p and explain how exponent and coefficient control domain, symmetry, end behavior, and growth.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of large-input comparison, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.
