## P4.2. Polynomial functions, degree, and leading term

**Lesson outcome.** Identify degree, leading term, coefficients, and standard form, and explain why the leading term governs large-input behavior.

### Opening

A polynomial is a finite sum of nonnegative integer powers, and its leading term eventually dominates.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use polynomial functions, degree, and leading term to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Write standard form, identify degree and leading coefficient, and compare the full polynomial with its leading term for large |x|.

Leading-term behavior is long-range and does not determine local zeros or turns.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through polynomial anatomy, leading-term ratio, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is replacing the polynomial by its leading term near the origin.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** For 7-2x^4+5x^2.

**Solution.** Standard form -2x^4+5x^2+7; degree 4; leading coefficient -2.

**Interpretation.** Missing powers have zero coefficients.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Leading term of -4x^7+x^9-1.

**Solution.** x^9.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses polynomial functions, degree, and leading term in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Is sqrt(x)+x polynomial?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Leading-term behavior is long-range and does not determine local zeros or turns.

*Caption:* Leading-term ratio: visual evidence for identify degree, leading term, coefficients, and standard form, and explain why the leading term governs large-input behavior.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of leading-term ratio, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

*Caption:* Local versus long-range view: visual evidence for identify degree, leading term, coefficients, and standard form, and explain why the leading term governs large-input behavior.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of local versus long-range view, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P4.3. End behavior

**Lesson outcome.** Determine polynomial end behavior from degree parity and leading coefficient and express it verbally, graphically, and with limit-style notation.

### Opening

Polynomial end behavior is determined by degree parity and leading coefficient sign.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use end behavior to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Identify total degree and leading sign, then state both left and right directions.

Even degree gives same-direction ends; odd degree gives opposite-direction ends.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through four end families, factored degree inventory, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is stating only one end or reading behavior from the constant term.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Determine end behavior of -3x^5+x^2.

**Solution.** Left end rises and right end falls.

**Interpretation.** The leading term is negative odd.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** End behavior of -x^4+2.

**Solution.** Both ends fall.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses end behavior in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Can even degree have opposite ends?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Even degree gives same-direction ends; odd degree gives opposite-direction ends.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of four end families, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

*Caption:* Factored degree inventory: visual evidence for determine polynomial end behavior from degree parity and leading coefficient and express it verbally, graphically, and with limit-style notation.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of factored degree inventory, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P4.3-V3 - Plausible graph filter.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is stating only one end or reading behavior from the constant term. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

## P4.4. Zeros, factors, and intercepts

**Lesson outcome.** Connect polynomial zeros, linear factors, solutions, and x-intercepts, and distinguish exact from approximate zeros.

### Opening

For a polynomial, P(r)=0, factor x-r, and intercept (r,0) describe the same feature.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use zeros, factors, and intercepts to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Factor when possible, solve each factor, and distinguish exact roots from numerical estimates.

A graph window can miss roots, and a complex zero is not a real x-intercept.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through zero-factor-intercept triangle, exact versus approximate roots, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is reporting an intercept point as a zero or treating a decimal as exact.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Zeros of x^3-4x.

**Solution.** x=-2,0,2.

**Interpretation.** Factoring exposes all real intercepts.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Factor for zero 7.

**Solution.** x-7.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses zeros, factors, and intercepts in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Intercept for zero -3.

**Solution.** (-3,0).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A graph window can miss roots, and a complex zero is not a real x-intercept.

## P4.5. Multiplicity and sign behavior

**Lesson outcome.** Relate the multiplicity of a zero to sign changes, crossing or touching behavior, and local flattening.

### Opening

Multiplicity is the exponent of a repeated zero factor and controls local sign behavior.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use multiplicity and sign behavior to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Odd multiplicity crosses and changes sign; even multiplicity touches and preserves sign; larger multiplicity flattens.

Multiplicity counts toward total degree even when several counts share one intercept.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through multiplicity gallery, factor sign table, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is assuming every intercept is a simple crossing.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze (x+2)^2(x-1)^3.

**Solution.** -2 touches; 1 crosses with flattening.

**Interpretation.** Even and odd multiplicities behave differently.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Behavior multiplicity 2.

**Solution.** Touches and turns.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses multiplicity and sign behavior in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Does even multiplicity change sign?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Multiplicity counts toward total degree even when several counts share one intercept.

### Figure storyboard

*Caption:* Multiplicity gallery: visual evidence for relate the multiplicity of a zero to sign changes, crossing or touching behavior, and local flattening.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of multiplicity gallery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P4.5-V2 - Factor sign table.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Odd multiplicity crosses and changes sign; even multiplicity touches and preserves sign; larger multiplicity flattens. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Factor sign table: visual evidence for relate the multiplicity of a zero to sign changes, crossing or touching behavior, and local flattening.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of factor sign table, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P4.5-V3 - Degree accounting.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is assuming every intercept is a simple crossing. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of degree accounting, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P4.6. Graph construction from structure

**Lesson outcome.** Construct a polynomial graph from degree, leading coefficient, zeros, multiplicities, sign intervals, and selected points.

### Opening

A structural polynomial sketch begins with ends, zeros, multiplicities, signs, and selected values.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use graph construction from structure to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Determine end behavior, mark zeros and crossing type, test interval signs, compute the y-intercept, and connect smoothly.

Polynomials are continuous and cannot contain holes, jumps, or asymptotes.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through polynomial graphing workflow, aligned sign chart, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

**Solution.** Positive.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses graph construction from structure in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

## P4.7. Turning points and local versus global behavior

**Lesson outcome.** Use degree bounds, graph evidence, and function values to analyze turning points and extrema.

### Opening

A turning point changes the graph from increasing to decreasing or the reverse.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use turning points and local versus global behavior to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Use the degree-n maximum of n-1 turns and compare local extrema with end behavior to identify absolute extrema.

Odd-degree polynomials are unbounded above and below; positive even-degree polynomials have an absolute minimum.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through turning-point gallery, local versus absolute extrema, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is confusing an x-intercept with a turning point.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Can degree 4 have five turns?

**Solution.** No; maximum 3.

**Interpretation.** Degree bounds graph complexity.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Max turns degree 7.

**Solution.** 6.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses turning points and local versus global behavior in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Does every zero create a turn?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Odd-degree polynomials are unbounded above and below; positive even-degree polynomials have an absolute minimum.

## P4.8. Intermediate Value reasoning and root bounds

**Lesson outcome.** Use polynomial continuity and sign changes to guarantee roots and bracket them within intervals.

### Opening

Polynomial continuity guarantees that every intermediate output between P(a) and P(b) occurs.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use intermediate value reasoning and root bounds to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Find opposite endpoint signs, conclude a zero exists, and refine the bracket by midpoint testing.

Opposite signs guarantee at least one root but not uniqueness; same signs do not prove no root.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through continuity crossing, bisection tree, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is treating a sign-change interval as proof of exactly one root.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** For x^3-x-1 on (1,2).

**Solution.** P(1)=-1 and P(2)=5, so a root lies in (1,2).

**Interpretation.** Continuity forces a crossing.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Can double root show no sign change?

**Solution.** Yes.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses intermediate value reasoning and root bounds in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Why continuity matters?

**Solution.** A discontinuous graph can jump across zero.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Opposite signs guarantee at least one root but not uniqueness; same signs do not prove no root.

### Figure storyboard

**P4.8-V1 - Continuity crossing.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: For x^3-x-1 on (1,2). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: P(1)=-1 and P(2)=5, so a root lies in (1,2).

*Caption:* Continuity crossing: visual evidence for use polynomial continuity and sign changes to guarantee roots and bracket them within intervals.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of continuity crossing, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P4.8-V2 - Bisection tree.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Find opposite endpoint signs, conclude a zero exists, and refine the bracket by midpoint testing. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Bisection tree: visual evidence for use polynomial continuity and sign changes to guarantee roots and bracket them within intervals.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of bisection tree, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P4.9. Polynomial division

**Lesson outcome.** Divide polynomials using long and synthetic division and verify the division algorithm P=DQ+R.

### Opening

Polynomial division produces quotient and remainder satisfying P=DQ+R.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use polynomial division to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Write standard form with zero placeholders, divide leading terms, multiply, subtract, repeat, and verify.

The remainder degree must be smaller than the divisor degree; synthetic division is limited to x-c in its basic form.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through long-division layout, synthetic correspondence, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is omitting missing powers or distributing subtraction incorrectly.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Divide x^3+2x^2-5x-6 by x+3.

**Solution.** Quotient x^2-x-2, remainder 0.

**Interpretation.** Zero remainder confirms a factor.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** c=-3 corresponds to divisor.

**Solution.** x+3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses polynomial division in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Remainder degree condition.

**Solution.** degree R < degree D.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The remainder degree must be smaller than the divisor degree; synthetic division is limited to x-c in its basic form.

## P4.10. Remainder and factor theorems

**Lesson outcome.** Use P(c) as the remainder on division by x-c and test whether x-c is a factor.

### Opening

The remainder on division by x-c is P(c), and x-c is a factor exactly when P(c)=0.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use remainder and factor theorems to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Evaluate the candidate or use synthetic division, then divide confirmed factors out and continue.

Repeated successful division by the same factor reveals multiplicity.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through remainder theorem substitution, factor-test loop, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is calling every rational candidate a factor before checking the remainder.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Is x+2 a factor of x^3-3x+2?

**Solution.** Yes, because P(-2)=0.

**Interpretation.** Zero remainder confirms the factor.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** If P(4)=7, remainder by x-4.

**Solution.** 7.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses remainder and factor theorems in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** If P(-2)=0, factor.

**Solution.** x+2.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Repeated successful division by the same factor reveals multiplicity.

## P4.11. Rational-root candidates and root search

**Lesson outcome.** Generate all possible rational zeros of an integer-coefficient polynomial and organize an efficient exact-and-numerical search.

### Opening

The Rational Root Theorem produces a finite list of possible rational zeros p/q.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use rational-root candidates and root search to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Let p divide the constant term and q divide the leading coefficient, reduce all signed ratios, and test candidates exactly.

The theorem limits rational possibilities only; irrational and complex roots may remain.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through rational-root lattice, candidate workflow, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is treating the candidate list as the solution list or omitting fractional candidates.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Candidates for 2x^3+x^2-8x-4.

**Solution.** ±1,±2,±4,±1/2.

**Interpretation.** The leading coefficient creates fractional candidates.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** If constant term zero.

**Solution.** Factor out x.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses rational-root candidates and root search in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The theorem limits rational possibilities only; irrational and complex roots may remain.

## P4.12. Complex zeros and the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra

**Lesson outcome.** Count polynomial zeros over the complex numbers, use conjugate pairs for real coefficients, and build real polynomials from complex roots.

### Opening

A degree-n polynomial has n complex zeros counted with multiplicity.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use complex zeros and the fundamental theorem of algebra to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

For real coefficients, add conjugate roots, form real quadratic factors, and count all root slots.

Nonreal zeros do not appear as real intercepts but are required for complete factorization.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through complex-root inventory, conjugate reflection, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is allowing one isolated nonreal root in a real-coefficient polynomial.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Zeros 3±2i.

**Solution.** Real factor x^2-6x+13.

**Interpretation.** Conjugate multiplication cancels imaginary terms.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Conjugate of 4+3i.

**Solution.** 4-3i.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses complex zeros and the fundamental theorem of algebra in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Factor for zeros ±2i.

**Solution.** x^2+4.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Nonreal zeros do not appear as real intercepts but are required for complete factorization.

## P4.13. Building polynomial models and finding numerical roots

**Lesson outcome.** Construct polynomial functions from conditions, fit vertical scale, and approximate roots that lack convenient exact forms.

### Opening

A polynomial model can be built from zeros, multiplicities, end behavior, and one scale-setting condition.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use building polynomial models and finding numerical roots to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor polynomial expressions.
2. Read zeros and graph behavior.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate forms.

### Exact learner exposition

Write a product a∏(x-r)^m, use end behavior for degree and sign, solve a from a point, and approximate any remaining roots with justified numerical evidence.

A sign-change search can miss even-multiplicity roots.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through condition-to-factor builder, numerical root refinement, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is expanding before the zero structure has been verified.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Zeros -1 and 2 double, P(0)=8.

**Solution.** P=2(x+1)(x-2)^2.

**Interpretation.** The point fixes vertical scale.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Real roots 2 and 1±i.

**Solution.** (x-2)(x^2-2x+2).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses building polynomial models and finding numerical roots in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Why keep factored form?

**Solution.** Preserves zeros and multiplicities.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A sign-change search can miss even-multiplicity roots.

### Figure storyboard

**P4.13-V1 - Condition-to-factor builder.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Zeros -1 and 2 double, P(0)=8. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: P=2(x+1)(x-2)^2.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of condition-to-factor builder, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P4.13-V2 - Numerical root refinement.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Write a product a∏(x-r)^m, use end behavior for degree and sign, solve a from a point, and approximate any remaining roots with justified numerical evidence. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Numerical root refinement: visual evidence for construct polynomial functions from conditions, fit vertical scale, and approximate roots that lack convenient exact forms.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of numerical root refinement, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## Unit investigation

The investigation for Polynomial Functions combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

## Unit review and mastery

# Unit P5: Rational Functions

**Governing question.** How does division create restrictions, discontinuities, asymptotes, and separated branches of behavior?

This unit studies how polynomial quotients create missing inputs and separated graph branches.

## Unit lessons

## P5.1. Rational functions and their domains

**Lesson outcome.** Identify rational functions and determine all excluded inputs from the original denominator before simplifying.

### Opening

A rational function P/Q excludes every real input where the original denominator Q is zero.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use rational functions and their domains to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Factor the denominator, solve Q=0, record exclusions, and partition the domain into intervals of continuity.

A rational function can have all-real domain when its denominator has no real zero.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through numerator-denominator role map, domain intervals, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is simplifying before recording the original exclusions.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Domain of x/(x^2-x-6).

**Solution.** x≠3,-2.

**Interpretation.** The denominator creates three continuity intervals.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Domain of (x-2)/(x^2-9).

**Solution.** x≠±3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses rational functions and their domains in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Do numerator zeros restrict domain?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A rational function can have all-real domain when its denominator has no real zero.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of numerator-denominator role map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.1-V2 - Domain intervals.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Factor the denominator, solve Q=0, record exclusions, and partition the domain into intervals of continuity. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Domain intervals: visual evidence for identify rational functions and determine all excluded inputs from the original denominator before simplifying.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of domain intervals, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.1-V3 - No-real-zero denominator.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is simplifying before recording the original exclusions. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* No-real-zero denominator: visual evidence for identify rational functions and determine all excluded inputs from the original denominator before simplifying.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of no-real-zero denominator, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P5.2. Equivalent formulas, different functions, and holes

**Lesson outcome.** Identify removable discontinuities caused by common factors and distinguish formula equivalence from function equality.

### Opening

A common factor creates a removable discontinuity: the original function follows the simplified curve but omits one point.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use equivalent formulas, different functions, and holes to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Factor, retain original exclusions, cancel, and evaluate the simplified formula at the cancelled input to locate the hole.

Two formulas define the same function only when both domains and values match.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through cancellation with domain memory, hole coordinate construction, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is filling the hole because the simplified formula has a value there.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze (x^2-9)/(x-3).

**Solution.** Line y=x+3 with hole (3,6).

**Interpretation.** The original denominator excludes 3.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Hole of (x^2+3x+2)/(x+1).

**Solution.** (-1,1).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses equivalent formulas, different functions, and holes in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Can a function have two holes?

**Solution.** Yes.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Two formulas define the same function only when both domains and values match.

### Figure storyboard

**P5.2-V1 - Cancellation with domain memory.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Analyze (x^2-9)/(x-3). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Line y=x+3 with hole (3,6).

*Caption:* Cancellation with domain memory: visual evidence for identify removable discontinuities caused by common factors and distinguish formula equivalence from function equality.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of cancellation with domain memory, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.2-V2 - Hole coordinate construction.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Factor, retain original exclusions, cancel, and evaluate the simplified formula at the cancelled input to locate the hole. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Hole coordinate construction: visual evidence for identify removable discontinuities caused by common factors and distinguish formula equivalence from function equality.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of hole coordinate construction, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.2-V3 - Equal expressions versus functions.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is filling the hole because the simplified formula has a value there. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Equal expressions versus functions: visual evidence for identify removable discontinuities caused by common factors and distinguish formula equivalence from function equality.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of equal expressions versus functions, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

</details>

9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. (-1,1).
3. Yes.
4. Simplified formula at excluded x.

6. Two formulas define the same function only when both domains and values match.
7. A frequent error is filling the hole because the simplified formula has a value there.

## P5.3. Vertical asymptotes and local behavior

**Lesson outcome.** Determine vertical asymptotes and describe one-sided behavior from factor signs and multiplicity.

### Opening

An uncancelled denominator zero often creates a vertical asymptote with unbounded one-sided behavior.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use vertical asymptotes and local behavior to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Simplify common factors, identify remaining denominator zeros, and use local factor signs and multiplicity to state each side.

Odd denominator multiplicity changes sign; even multiplicity preserves sign.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through one-sided arrows, multiplicity comparison, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is treating an asymptote as a function point or assuming both sides behave alike.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze 1/(x-2) near 2.

**Solution.** Left -∞, right +∞.

**Interpretation.** The first-power denominator changes sign.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** VAs of 1/(x^2-9).

**Solution.** x=±3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses vertical asymptotes and local behavior in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Does fully cancelled zero create VA?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Odd denominator multiplicity changes sign; even multiplicity preserves sign.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of multiplicity comparison, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of local sign ledger, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

7. A frequent error is treating an asymptote as a function point or assuming both sides behave alike.

## P5.4. Horizontal asymptotes and end behavior

**Lesson outcome.** Determine horizontal asymptotes from degree comparison and interpret them as long-run output behavior.

### Opening

Horizontal asymptotes describe rational end behavior through leading-degree comparison.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use horizontal asymptotes and end behavior to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Use y=0 when numerator degree is lower, leading coefficient ratio when degrees match, and division when numerator degree is higher.

Horizontal asymptotes can be crossed at finite inputs.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through degree comparison chart, leading-term quotient, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is applying the equal-degree ratio to unequal degrees.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze (5x^3-2)/(2x^3+x).

**Solution.** Horizontal asymptote y=5/2.

**Interpretation.** Equal degrees use leading coefficient ratio.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** HA of (3x^2+1)/(x^2-4).

**Solution.** y=3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses horizontal asymptotes and end behavior in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Does (x^3+1)/(x^2+1) have HA?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Horizontal asymptotes can be crossed at finite inputs.

### Figure storyboard

**P5.4-V1 - Degree comparison chart.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Analyze (5x^3-2)/(2x^3+x). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Horizontal asymptote y=5/2.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of degree comparison chart, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.4-V2 - Leading-term quotient.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Use y=0 when numerator degree is lower, leading coefficient ratio when degrees match, and division when numerator degree is higher. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Leading-term quotient: visual evidence for determine horizontal asymptotes from degree comparison and interpret them as long-run output behavior.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of leading-term quotient, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.4-V3 - Asymptote crossing.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is applying the equal-degree ratio to unequal degrees. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of asymptote crossing, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P5.5. Slant and polynomial asymptotes

**Lesson outcome.** Use polynomial division to determine nonhorizontal asymptotes and describe the shrinking remainder term.

### Opening

Polynomial division reveals a nonhorizontal asymptote when a rational numerator has higher degree.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use slant and polynomial asymptotes to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Write R=Q+remainder/divisor; the graph approaches y=Q as the proper remainder term approaches zero.

Degree difference one gives a slant line; larger differences can give higher-degree asymptotes.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through division decomposition, slant approach, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is claiming the rational function equals the quotient everywhere.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze (x^2+1)/(x-1).

**Solution.** x+1+2/(x-1), so asymptote y=x+1.

**Interpretation.** The remainder measures the gap.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Polynomial asymptote of (x^3+1)/x.

**Solution.** y=x^2.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses slant and polynomial asymptotes in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** When slant occurs.

**Solution.** Degree difference 1.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Degree difference one gives a slant line; larger differences can give higher-degree asymptotes.

### Figure storyboard

**P5.5-V1 - Division decomposition.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Analyze (x^2+1)/(x-1). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: x+1+2/(x-1), so asymptote y=x+1.

*Caption:* Division decomposition: visual evidence for use polynomial division to determine nonhorizontal asymptotes and describe the shrinking remainder term.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of division decomposition, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.5-V2 - Slant approach.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Write R=Q+remainder/divisor; the graph approaches y=Q as the proper remainder term approaches zero. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Slant approach: visual evidence for use polynomial division to determine nonhorizontal asymptotes and describe the shrinking remainder term.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of slant approach, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.5-V3 - Polynomial asymptote.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is claiming the rational function equals the quotient everywhere. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Polynomial asymptote: visual evidence for use polynomial division to determine nonhorizontal asymptotes and describe the shrinking remainder term.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of polynomial asymptote, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

</details>

### Practice

2. Polynomial asymptote of (x^3+1)/x.

4. Can graph cross it?
5. State the defining idea of slant and polynomial asymptotes in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

2. y=x^2.
3. Degree difference 1.
4. Yes, if remainder is zero at an allowed input.
5. Polynomial division reveals a nonhorizontal asymptote when a rational numerator has higher degree.
6. Degree difference one gives a slant line; larger differences can give higher-degree asymptotes.
7. A frequent error is claiming the rational function equals the quotient everywhere.

9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P5.6. Intercepts and sign charts

**Lesson outcome.** Find rational intercepts and determine positive and negative intervals using factored sign structure.

### Opening

A rational function has constant sign between numerator zeros and denominator zeros.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use intercepts and sign charts to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Factor, classify zeros as intercepts, holes, or asymptotes, test one input per interval, and calculate the y-intercept if allowed.

Even multiplicity preserves sign; odd multiplicity changes sign.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through rational sign chart, intercept versus hole, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is counting a cancelled numerator zero as an intercept.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze signs of (x-1)/(x+2).

**Solution.** Positive, negative, positive across -2 and 1.

**Interpretation.** Critical values split the sign intervals.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Y-intercept.

**Solution.** (0,-5).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses intercepts and sign charts in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Critical values of x/[(x-2)(x+4)].

**Solution.** -4,0,2.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Even multiplicity preserves sign; odd multiplicity changes sign.

### Figure storyboard

**P5.6-V1 - Rational sign chart.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Analyze signs of (x-1)/(x+2). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Positive, negative, positive across -2 and 1.

*Caption:* Rational sign chart: visual evidence for find rational intercepts and determine positive and negative intervals using factored sign structure.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of rational sign chart, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.6-V2 - Intercept versus hole.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Factor, classify zeros as intercepts, holes, or asymptotes, test one input per interval, and calculate the y-intercept if allowed. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Intercept versus hole: visual evidence for find rational intercepts and determine positive and negative intervals using factored sign structure.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of intercept versus hole, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.6-V3 - Multiplicity at rational zero.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is counting a cancelled numerator zero as an intercept. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Multiplicity at rational zero: visual evidence for find rational intercepts and determine positive and negative intervals using factored sign structure.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of multiplicity at rational zero, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

(5,0).

</details>

### Practice

2. Y-intercept.
3. Critical values of x/[(x-2)(x+4)].

6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. (0,-5).
3. -4,0,2.
4. No.

7. A frequent error is counting a cancelled numerator zero as an intercept.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.

## P5.7. Complete rational graph construction

**Lesson outcome.** Construct a complete rational graph using domain, discontinuities, asymptotes, intercepts, signs, and selected values.

### Opening

A complete rational graph combines domain, holes, vertical and end asymptotes, intercepts, signs, and selected points.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use complete rational graph construction to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Build a feature ledger, sketch each continuity interval separately, state one-sided behavior, and verify with technology.

A graph must not connect across an excluded input, and range claims may require algebra.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through complete checklist, branch assembly, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is omitting a hole or connecting branches across a vertical asymptote.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze (x+1)/(x-2).

**Solution.** VA x=2, HA y=1, x-intercept -1, y-intercept -1/2, signs +,-,+.

**Interpretation.** The features determine two branches.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Can HA be crossed?

**Solution.** Yes.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses complete rational graph construction in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Can VA be crossed?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A graph must not connect across an excluded input, and range claims may require algebra.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of complete checklist, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of branch assembly, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P5.8. Rational equations and function intersections

**Lesson outcome.** Solve rational equations and intersections exactly or numerically while rejecting excluded candidates.

### Opening

Rational intersections satisfy f(x)=g(x), but denominator clearing creates candidates that must be checked in the original domains.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use rational equations and function intersections to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Record restrictions, multiply every term by an LCD, solve the resulting equation, and reject excluded candidates.

An excluded candidate can arise because the clearing multiplier is zero where the original equation is undefined.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through intersection equation, candidate filter, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is accepting every solution of the cleared equation.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve (x+1)/(x-2)=3/(x-2).

**Solution.** Candidate x=2 is excluded, so no solution.

**Interpretation.** The transformed equation exists where the original does not.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Solve 2/x=1/(x-3).

**Solution.** x=6.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses rational equations and function intersections in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** X-intercept of (x-2)/(x+3).

**Solution.** x=2.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: An excluded candidate can arise because the clearing multiplier is zero where the original equation is undefined.

### Figure storyboard

**P5.8-V1 - Intersection equation.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Solve (x+1)/(x-2)=3/(x-2). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Candidate x=2 is excluded, so no solution.

*Caption:* Intersection equation: visual evidence for solve rational equations and intersections exactly or numerically while rejecting excluded candidates.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of intersection equation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.8-V2 - Candidate filter.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Record restrictions, multiply every term by an LCD, solve the resulting equation, and reject excluded candidates. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Candidate filter: visual evidence for solve rational equations and intersections exactly or numerically while rejecting excluded candidates.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of candidate filter, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

*Caption:* Exact-numerical method tree: visual evidence for solve rational equations and intersections exactly or numerically while rejecting excluded candidates.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of exact-numerical method tree, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P5.9. Rational inequalities

**Lesson outcome.** Solve rational inequalities using critical values, sign intervals, and correct endpoint inclusion.

### Opening

Rational inequalities are solved by critical-value sign intervals rather than unsafe cross multiplication.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use rational inequalities to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Move all terms to one side, combine and factor, list numerator and denominator zeros, test intervals, and apply endpoint rules.

Allowed numerator zeros may be included for non-strict inequalities; denominator zeros never are.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through inequality sign chart, endpoint rules, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is multiplying by a denominator whose sign changes across the domain.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve (x+3)/(x-4)≤0.

**Solution.** [-3,4).

**Interpretation.** Include the zero, exclude the asymptote.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Solve 1/x<0.

**Solution.** (-∞,0).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses rational inequalities in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Which endpoints may be included?

**Solution.** Allowed numerator zeros.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Allowed numerator zeros may be included for non-strict inequalities; denominator zeros never are.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of inequality sign chart, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.9-V2 - Endpoint rules.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Move all terms to one side, combine and factor, list numerator and denominator zeros, test intervals, and apply endpoint rules. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Endpoint rules: visual evidence for solve rational inequalities using critical values, sign intervals, and correct endpoint inclusion.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of endpoint rules, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.9-V3 - Function comparison.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is multiplying by a denominator whose sign changes across the domain. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Function comparison: visual evidence for solve rational inequalities using critical values, sign intervals, and correct endpoint inclusion.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of function comparison, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

(-5,2).

</details>

### Practice

2. Solve 1/x<0.

4. Which always excluded?

8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

2. (-∞,0).
3. Allowed numerator zeros.
4. Denominator zeros.
5. Rational inequalities are solved by critical-value sign intervals rather than unsafe cross multiplication.
6. Allowed numerator zeros may be included for non-strict inequalities; denominator zeros never are.
7. A frequent error is multiplying by a denominator whose sign changes across the domain.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.

## P5.10. Variation and rational models

**Lesson outcome.** Build and interpret direct, inverse, joint, and combined variation models with meaningful parameters and domains.

### Opening

Variation language describes direct, inverse, joint, and combined proportional structure.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use variation and rational models to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Translate the wording into a formula with constant k, use a known data point to find k, and interpret how output responds to input changes.

Inverse models exclude zero and often use positive contextual domains. Inverse variation is not an inverse function.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through variation relationship map, positive-domain inverse model, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is placing a factor in the numerator or denominator contrary to the wording.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** y varies inversely with x; y=5 at x=8.

**Solution.** y=40/x.

**Interpretation.** The product xy remains constant.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Inverse y=3 at x=10.

**Solution.** y=30/x.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses variation and rational models in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** If x doubles in k/x.

**Solution.** y halves.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Inverse models exclude zero and often use positive contextual domains. Inverse variation is not an inverse function.

### Figure storyboard

The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

## P5.11. Partial-fraction structure

**Lesson outcome.** Decompose proper rational functions with factorable denominators into simpler component fractions and verify recombination.

### Opening

Partial fractions rewrite a proper rational function as a sum of simpler components determined by denominator factors.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use partial-fraction structure to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Factor numerator and denominator.
2. Preserve original restrictions.
3. Use sign and asymptotic notation.

### Exact learner exposition

Divide first if needed, factor the denominator, write every required component, clear denominators, solve coefficients, and recombine to verify.

Repeated linear factors need every power; irreducible quadratics need a linear numerator.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through denominator-component map, strategic substitution, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is omitting a repeated-power term.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Decompose 1/[x(x+1)].

**Solution.** 1/x-1/(x+1).

**Interpretation.** Recombination returns the original fraction.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Is (x^2+1)/(x-1) proper?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses partial-fraction structure in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** What first if improper?

**Solution.** Polynomial division.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Repeated linear factors need every power; irreducible quadratics need a linear numerator.

### Figure storyboard

**P5.11-V1 - Denominator-component map.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Decompose 1/[x(x+1)]. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: 1/x-1/(x+1).

*Caption:* Denominator-component map: visual evidence for decompose proper rational functions with factorable denominators into simpler component fractions and verify recombination.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of denominator-component map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.11-V2 - Strategic substitution.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Divide first if needed, factor the denominator, write every required component, clear denominators, solve coefficients, and recombine to verify. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Strategic substitution: visual evidence for decompose proper rational functions with factorable denominators into simpler component fractions and verify recombination.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of strategic substitution, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P5.11-V3 - Recombination check.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is omitting a repeated-power term. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Recombination check: visual evidence for decompose proper rational functions with factorable denominators into simpler component fractions and verify recombination.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of recombination check, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

<details><summary>Check</summary>

</details>

### Practice

2. Is (x^2+1)/(x-1) proper?
3. What first if improper?
4. Can decomposition restore exclusions?
5. State the defining idea of partial-fraction structure in one precise sentence.
6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.
7. Describe the first invalid step in the common error.
8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

### Practice answers

1. A/(x-1)+B/(x+3).
2. No.
3. Polynomial division.
4. No.
5. Partial fractions rewrite a proper rational function as a sum of simpler components determined by denominator factors.
6. Repeated linear factors need every power; irreducible quadratics need a linear numerator.
7. A frequent error is omitting a repeated-power term.
8. Translate the answer into a graph, table, interval, mapping, equation, or context and verify that the same inputs, outputs, and restrictions appear.
9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## Unit investigation

The investigation for Rational Functions combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

## Unit review and mastery

The review mixes current-unit questions with retrieval from earlier units. The mastery assessment balances symbolic fluency, graph and table interpretation, explanation, error analysis, and modeling transfer.


\pagebreak

# Unit P6: Exponential and Logarithmic Functions

**Governing question.** How do functions model constant proportional change, and how can exponentiation be reversed?

This unit studies repeated proportional change and the logarithmic inverse of exponentiation.

## Unit lessons

## P6.1. Additive versus multiplicative change

**Lesson outcome.** Distinguish linear, exponential, and neither patterns by comparing equal-input differences, ratios, and percent changes.

### Opening

Constant first differences indicate additive linear change; constant ratios indicate multiplicative exponential change.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use additive versus multiplicative change to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Compare equal input steps, compute differences and ratios, and convert percentage rates to multipliers.

Noisy data suggest a candidate family rather than proving one.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through difference-ratio comparison, additive-multiplicative timelines, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is treating a constant percentage as a constant absolute amount.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Values 5,15,45,135.

**Solution.** Exponential ratio 3.

**Interpretation.** Each step triples.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Classify 2,6,18,54.

**Solution.** Exponential.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses additive versus multiplicative change in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Multiplier for 7% growth.

**Solution.** 1.07.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Noisy data suggest a candidate family rather than proving one.

### Figure storyboard

**P6.1-V1 - Difference-ratio comparison.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Values 5,15,45,135. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Exponential ratio 3.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of difference-ratio comparison, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.1-V2 - Additive-multiplicative timelines.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Compare equal input steps, compute differences and ratios, and convert percentage rates to multipliers. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Additive-multiplicative timelines: visual evidence for distinguish linear, exponential, and neither patterns by comparing equal-input differences, ratios, and percent changes.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of additive-multiplicative timelines, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.1-V3 - Early similarity, late divergence.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is treating a constant percentage as a constant absolute amount. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Early similarity, late divergence: visual evidence for distinguish linear, exponential, and neither patterns by comparing equal-input differences, ratios, and percent changes.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of early similarity, late divergence, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

</details>

4. Multiplier for 18% decay.

6. State the condition that must be checked before using the method.

8. Explain how the result should be checked in another representation.
9. Write one exact conclusion and one corresponding approximate conclusion.
10. State how this lesson connects forward.

2. Exponential.
3. 1.07.
4. 0.82.

6. Noisy data suggest a candidate family rather than proving one.
7. A frequent error is treating a constant percentage as a constant absolute amount.

## P6.2. Exponential functions and parameters

**Lesson outcome.** Analyze f(x)=ab^x by interpreting initial value, base, multiplier, percent rate, domain, range, and asymptote.

### Opening

In f(x)=ab^x, a is f(0) and b is the one-step output multiplier.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use exponential functions and parameters to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Read the initial value, convert b to a growth or decay rate, and recover b from output ratios across equal input steps.

The real continuous exponential base is positive and not 1.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through exponential parameter dashboard, one-step multiplier proof, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is calling b itself the percentage rate.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Interpret 1200(1.04)^t.

**Solution.** Initial 1200 and 4% growth per year.

**Interpretation.** The multiplier is 1.04.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Rate for base 0.64.

**Solution.** 36% decay.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses exponential functions and parameters in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Domain of 5(0.4)^x.

**Solution.** All real numbers.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The real continuous exponential base is positive and not 1.

### Figure storyboard

**P6.2-V1 - Exponential parameter dashboard.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Interpret 1200(1.04)^t. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Initial 1200 and 4% growth per year.
