*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of exponential parameter dashboard, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.2-V2 - One-step multiplier proof.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Read the initial value, convert b to a growth or decay rate, and recover b from output ratios across equal input steps. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* One-step multiplier proof: visual evidence for analyze f(x)=ab^x by interpreting initial value, base, multiplier, percent rate, domain, range, and asymptote.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of one-step multiplier proof, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.2-V3 - Growth-decay family.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is calling b itself the percentage rate. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Growth-decay family: visual evidence for analyze f(x)=ab^x by interpreting initial value, base, multiplier, percent rate, domain, range, and asymptote.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of growth-decay family, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P6.3. Exponential transformations

**Lesson outcome.** Analyze transformed exponential functions and track asymptotes, domain, range, and feature points.

### Opening

Transformed exponentials use the general input-output mapping, with horizontal asymptote y=k.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use exponential transformations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Map parent points, translate the asymptote, determine which side contains the range, and evaluate the actual y-intercept.

In a b^{c(x-h)}+k, a is not necessarily f(0) after a horizontal shift.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through transformed anchors, range orientation, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is leaving the asymptote at zero after a vertical translation.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze -3·2^(x-1)+4.

**Solution.** HA y=4, range (-∞,4), point (1,1).

**Interpretation.** Reflection and shift determine orientation.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Range of -2^x+1.

**Solution.** (-∞,1).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses exponential transformations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Rewrite 9^(x/2).

**Solution.** 3^x.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: In a b^{c(x-h)}+k, a is not necessarily f(0) after a horizontal shift.

### Figure storyboard

**P6.3-V1 - Transformed anchors.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Analyze -3·2^(x-1)+4. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: HA y=4, range (-∞,4), point (1,1).

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of transformed anchors, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

*Caption:* Range orientation: visual evidence for analyze transformed exponential functions and track asymptotes, domain, range, and feature points.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of range orientation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.3-V3 - Effective-base rewrite.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is leaving the asymptote at zero after a vertical translation. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Effective-base rewrite: visual evidence for analyze transformed exponential functions and track asymptotes, domain, range, and feature points.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of effective-base rewrite, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

5. Transformed exponentials use the general input-output mapping, with horizontal asymptote y=k.
6. In a b^{c(x-h)}+k, a is not necessarily f(0) after a horizontal shift.
7. A frequent error is leaving the asymptote at zero after a vertical translation.

9. Keep the symbolic form exact, then report a rounded value with its stated precision only when numerical comparison is needed.

## P6.4. Growth and decay models

**Lesson outcome.** Construct exponential models from an initial value, rate, multiplier, two points, or a known doubling or half-life.

### Opening

Exponential models can be written from an initial value and rate, multiplier, doubling time, or half-life.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use growth and decay models to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Choose the form matching the information, state the time unit and domain, and interpret the repeated multiplier.

Growth ignores resource limits and decay approaches zero without becoming negative.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through model-form selector, doubling-half-life timelines, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is mixing time units or using 6 instead of 1.06 for 6 percent growth.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** 240 mg with half-life 6 hours.

**Solution.** A(t)=240(1/2)^(t/6).

**Interpretation.** Each six-hour interval halves the amount.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** 900 decays 4%.

**Solution.** 900(0.96)^t.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses growth and decay models in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** 50 doubles every 8 days.

**Solution.** 50·2^(t/8).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Growth ignores resource limits and decay approaches zero without becoming negative.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of model-form selector, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.4-V2 - Doubling-half-life timelines.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Choose the form matching the information, state the time unit and domain, and interpret the repeated multiplier. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Doubling-half-life timelines: visual evidence for construct exponential models from an initial value, rate, multiplier, two points, or a known doubling or half-life.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of doubling-half-life timelines, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P6.5. Compound interest and effective rate

**Lesson outcome.** Use periodic compounding models and compare nominal rates, periodic rates, compounding frequency, and effective annual yield.

### Opening

Periodic compounding uses A=P(1+r/n)^(nt), where r/n is the periodic rate and nt counts periods.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use compound interest and effective rate to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Identify principal, nominal annual rate, compounding frequency, periodic rate, total periods, and effective annual multiplier.

The simple model omits fees, deposits, withdrawals, taxes, and changing rates unless included explicitly.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through compounding timeline, nominal versus effective rate, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is using r as the periodic rate or t as the number of periods.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** $2000 at 6% monthly for 3 years.

**Solution.** A=2000(1.005)^36≈2393.36.

**Interpretation.** There are 36 monthly periods.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Formula at 5% monthly.

**Solution.** P(1+0.05/12)^(12t).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses compound interest and effective rate in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Periods in 7 years monthly.

**Solution.** 84.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The simple model omits fees, deposits, withdrawals, taxes, and changing rates unless included explicitly.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of compounding timeline, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.5-V2 - Nominal versus effective rate.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Identify principal, nominal annual rate, compounding frequency, periodic rate, total periods, and effective annual multiplier. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Nominal versus effective rate: visual evidence for use periodic compounding models and compare nominal rates, periodic rates, compounding frequency, and effective annual yield.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of nominal versus effective rate, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P6.6. Continuous growth and the number e

**Lesson outcome.** Use A(t)=A0e^(kt) for continuous proportional change and interpret the continuous rate parameter k.

### Opening

The number e arises from the limit of increasingly frequent proportional compounding.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use continuous growth and the number e to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Use A=A0e^(kt), interpret e^k as the one-unit multiplier, and compare k with the effective one-unit rate.

k is a continuous rate parameter, not exactly the ordinary percent change.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through compounding limit to e, continuous parameter dashboard, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is interpreting k=0.06 as exactly 6 percent effective growth.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Interpret 80e^(-0.3t).

**Solution.** Continuous decay with one-unit multiplier e^-0.3≈0.7408.

**Interpretation.** The negative parameter reduces the amount.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Initial value of 7e^(3t).

**Solution.** 7.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses continuous growth and the number e in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Approximate e.

**Solution.** 2.71828.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: k is a continuous rate parameter, not exactly the ordinary percent change.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of compounding limit to e, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.6-V2 - Continuous parameter dashboard.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Use A=A0e^(kt), interpret e^k as the one-unit multiplier, and compare k with the effective one-unit rate. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of continuous parameter dashboard, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.6-V3 - Periodic versus continuous.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is interpreting k=0.06 as exactly 6 percent effective growth. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

## P6.7. Exponential regression and residuals

**Lesson outcome.** Fit an exponential model to data, interpret parameters, inspect residuals, and evaluate interpolation and extrapolation.

### Opening

Exponential regression estimates a repeated-change model from imperfect data, and residuals measure observed minus predicted output.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use exponential regression and residuals to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Fit parameters, interpret them, calculate residuals, inspect pattern, compare competitor families, and restrict extrapolation.

A high fit statistic does not prove causation or indefinite model validity.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through regression fit, residual pattern gallery, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is treating a regression formula as an exact law.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Regression y=12.4(1.18)^x.

**Solution.** Initial model value 12.4 and 18% growth per step.

**Interpretation.** Parameter meaning depends on whether x=0 is meaningful.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Interpret b=0.81.

**Solution.** 19% decay.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses exponential regression and residuals in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Good residual pattern.

**Solution.** Small and patternless.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A high fit statistic does not prove causation or indefinite model validity.

### Figure storyboard

**P6.7-V1 - Regression fit.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Regression y=12.4(1.18)^x. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: Initial model value 12.4 and 18% growth per step.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of regression fit, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.7-V2 - Residual pattern gallery.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Fit parameters, interpret them, calculate residuals, inspect pattern, compare competitor families, and restrict extrapolation. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Residual pattern gallery: visual evidence for fit an exponential model to data, interpret parameters, inspect residuals, and evaluate interpolation and extrapolation.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of residual pattern gallery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P6.8. Logistic and bounded growth

**Lesson outcome.** Analyze logistic growth qualitatively and interpret initial value, carrying capacity, growth rate, and inflection behavior.

### Opening

A logistic model grows rapidly at first and then slows toward a carrying capacity K.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use logistic and bounded growth to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Identify K, initial value, rate parameter, midpoint K/2, and the inflection where growth changes from accelerating to decelerating.

A fitted carrying capacity is an estimate and may change with the environment.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through exponential versus logistic, logistic anatomy, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is estimating K from early data that never show the upper bend.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** P=1000/[1+9e^(-0.4t)].

**Solution.** K=1000 and P(0)=100.

**Interpretation.** The model approaches its carrying capacity.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Output at inflection.

**Solution.** K/2.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses logistic and bounded growth in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** P(0) for 900/[1+8e^-t].

**Solution.** 100.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A fitted carrying capacity is an estimate and may change with the environment.

### Figure storyboard

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of exponential versus logistic, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.8-V2 - Logistic anatomy.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Identify K, initial value, rate parameter, midpoint K/2, and the inflection where growth changes from accelerating to decelerating. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of logistic anatomy, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P6.9. Logarithms as inverse functions

**Lesson outcome.** Define log base b as the inverse of b^x, convert between forms, and interpret logarithms as exponents.

### Opening

log_b(y)=x exactly when b^x=y; a logarithm is the exponent needed to produce a positive output.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use logarithms as inverse functions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Convert between forms, use exact powers, and swap exponential domain and range to obtain logarithmic features.

b>0, b≠1, and the logarithm argument is positive.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through inverse reflection, base-exponent-output triangle, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is confusing base, exponent, and output positions.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Evaluate log_3(1/27).

**Solution.** -3.

**Interpretation.** 3^-3=1/27.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** log_2(1).

**Solution.** 0.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses logarithms as inverse functions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Domain of log_b x.

**Solution.** (0,∞).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: b>0, b≠1, and the logarithm argument is positive.

10. State how this lesson connects forward.

## P6.10. Logarithmic graphs and transformations

**Lesson outcome.** Analyze transformed logarithmic functions, including domain, range, vertical asymptote, intercepts, and base-dependent direction.

### Opening

A transformed logarithm has a vertical input boundary where its argument equals zero.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use logarithmic graphs and transformations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Solve the positive-argument inequality, locate the asymptote, map parent points, and find intercepts by exponential conversion.

A negative inside coefficient places the domain on the opposite side of the asymptote.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through log parent features, transformed boundary, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is choosing the wrong side of the domain boundary.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Analyze log_3(4-2x).

**Solution.** Domain x<2 and VA x=2.

**Interpretation.** The negative inside coefficient places the graph to the left.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** VA log_2(x+4).

**Solution.** x=-4.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses logarithmic graphs and transformations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** X-intercept log_3(x-2).

**Solution.** x=3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A negative inside coefficient places the domain on the opposite side of the asymptote.

## P6.11. Logarithm laws

**Lesson outcome.** Derive and apply product, quotient, and power laws while preserving positive-argument domains.

### Opening

Logarithm laws convert products to sums, quotients to differences, and powers to coefficients.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use logarithm laws to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Factor multiplicative structure, apply the laws, and preserve positive-argument conditions.

No law separates a logarithm of a sum or difference.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through exponent-to-log derivation, valid-invalid law gallery, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is writing log(x+y)=logx+logy.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Expand log_b(5x^3/y).

**Solution.** log_b5+3log_bx-log_by.

**Interpretation.** Product, power, and quotient laws combine.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Expand log_b(x/y).

**Solution.** log_bx-log_by.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses logarithm laws in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Condense 3lnx-lny.

**Solution.** ln(x^3/y).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: No law separates a logarithm of a sum or difference.

## P6.12. Solving exponential equations

**Lesson outcome.** Solve exponential equations using common bases, logarithms, and numerical methods, and interpret exact and approximate answers.

### Opening

Exponential equations place the unknown in an exponent and are solved by common bases, logarithms, substitution, or numerical methods.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use solving exponential equations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Isolate the exponential, choose a common-base or logarithmic path, retain exact form, approximate at the end, and check the model domain.

Positive-base exponentials have positive outputs; sums of exponentials may require a new variable.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through equation method tree, taking logs steps, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is taking logs before isolating the exponential or rounding too early.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve 3^x=10.

**Solution.** x=ln10/ln3≈2.096.

**Interpretation.** The exact quotient precedes the approximation.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Solve 9^x=27.

**Solution.** x=3/2.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses solving exponential equations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Solve e^(3x)=7.

**Solution.** x=ln7/3.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Positive-base exponentials have positive outputs; sums of exponentials may require a new variable.

## P6.13. Solving logarithmic equations and interpreting inverse models

**Lesson outcome.** Solve logarithmic equations, reject invalid arguments, and use logarithms to analyze doubling time, half-life, and logarithmic scales.

### Opening

A logarithmic equation must be solved inside the original positive-argument domain.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use solving logarithmic equations and interpreting inverse models to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Use exponent laws.
2. Interpret function parameters.
3. Distinguish exact and approximate values.

### Exact learner exposition

Write domain conditions, condense when useful, convert to exponential form, solve, and reject invalid candidates.

Doubling time, half-life, and logarithmic scales are inverse questions for exponential models.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through log candidate filter, doubling-half-life view, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is accepting a root that makes an original logarithm argument nonpositive.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve ln x+ln(x-3)=ln4.

**Solution.** x=4; candidate -1 is invalid.

**Interpretation.** The domain x>3 filters the roots.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Solve ln(x-2)=0.

**Solution.** x=3.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses solving logarithmic equations and interpreting inverse models in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Factor for +1 on base-10 log scale.

**Solution.** 10.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Doubling time, half-life, and logarithmic scales are inverse questions for exponential models.

### Figure storyboard

**P6.13-V1 - Log candidate filter.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Solve ln x+ln(x-3)=ln4. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: x=4; candidate -1 is invalid.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of log candidate filter, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P6.13-V2 - Doubling-half-life view.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Write domain conditions, condense when useful, convert to exponential form, solve, and reject invalid candidates. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Doubling-half-life view: visual evidence for solve logarithmic equations, reject invalid arguments, and use logarithms to analyze doubling time, half-life, and logarithmic scales.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of doubling-half-life view, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

The investigation for Exponential and Logarithmic Functions combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

# Unit P7: Systems, Matrices, and Multivariable Models

**Governing question.** How can several relationships and variables be organized, solved, and interpreted together?

This unit expands from one relationship to simultaneous constraints and matrix transformations.

## Unit lessons

## P7.1. Systems as intersections and models

**Lesson outcome.** Interpret a system solution as an input-output point satisfying every equation and solve two-variable systems graphically and algebraically.

### Opening

A system solution satisfies every equation simultaneously and appears graphically as a common intersection.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use systems as intersections and models to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Choose graphing, substitution, or elimination from the visible structure and verify the ordered pair in every equation.

Parallel lines have no solution and coincident lines have infinitely many solutions.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through system representation triangle, line solution-count gallery, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is accepting a point that satisfies only one equation.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve y=2x+1 and y=-x+7.

**Solution.** (2,5).

**Interpretation.** The lines intersect once.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Solve 2x+y=7 and x-y=2.

**Solution.** (3,1).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses systems as intersections and models in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Check (1,3) in x+y=4,2x-y=-1.

**Solution.** It satisfies both.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Parallel lines have no solution and coincident lines have infinitely many solutions.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of system representation triangle, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P7.1-V2 - Line solution-count gallery.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Choose graphing, substitution, or elimination from the visible structure and verify the ordered pair in every equation. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Line solution-count gallery: visual evidence for interpret a system solution as an input-output point satisfying every equation and solve two-variable systems graphically and algebraically.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of line solution-count gallery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P7.1-V3 - Method-choice map.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is accepting a point that satisfies only one equation. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Caption:* Method-choice map: visual evidence for interpret a system solution as an input-output point satisfying every equation and solve two-variable systems graphically and algebraically.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of method-choice map, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P7.2. Nonlinear systems

**Lesson outcome.** Solve systems involving lines, quadratics, circles, rational, exponential, or other nonlinear functions and interpret all real intersections.

### Opening

A nonlinear system contains at least one curved relation and may have zero, one, or several intersections.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use nonlinear systems to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Substitute an isolated equation, solve the resulting one-variable equation, recover the other coordinate, and check every candidate.

A repeated root often represents tangency; a negative discriminant can show no real line-parabola intersections.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through nonlinear intersection gallery, substitution workflow, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is listing only x-values instead of complete intersection points.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve y=x^2 and y=2x-1.

**Solution.** (1,1).

**Interpretation.** The repeated root represents tangency.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Solve y=x^2 and y=-1.

**Solution.** No real solution.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses nonlinear systems in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Meaning double root.

**Solution.** Tangency.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A repeated root often represents tangency; a negative discriminant can show no real line-parabola intersections.

### Figure storyboard

**P7.2-V1 - Nonlinear intersection gallery.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Solve y=x^2 and y=2x-1. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: (1,1).

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of nonlinear intersection gallery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P7.2-V2 - Substitution workflow.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Substitute an isolated equation, solve the resulting one-variable equation, recover the other coordinate, and check every candidate. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

## P7.3. Systems of inequalities and feasible regions

**Lesson outcome.** Graph simultaneous inequalities, identify feasible regions, and interpret boundaries and vertices.

### Opening

A system of inequalities describes the intersection of several shaded solution regions.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use systems of inequalities and feasible regions to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Graph each boundary, shade the correct side, include contextual constraints, find vertices, and evaluate an objective when requested.

Solid boundaries are included; dashed boundaries are excluded. Feasible regions may be bounded, unbounded, or empty.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through layered half-planes, boundary inclusion, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is using the union of regions instead of their overlap.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** x,y≥0, x+y≤10, 2x+y≤14.

**Solution.** Vertices (0,0),(0,10),(4,6),(7,0).

**Interpretation.** The constraints form a feasible polygon.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Line style y>2x+1.

**Solution.** Dashed.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses systems of inequalities and feasible regions in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Intersection x+y=8,x+2y=10.

**Solution.** (6,2).

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Solid boundaries are included; dashed boundaries are excluded. Feasible regions may be bounded, unbounded, or empty.

## P7.4. Systems in three variables

**Lesson outcome.** Solve and classify three-variable linear systems and interpret their solutions as intersections of planes.

### Opening

A linear equation in three variables describes a plane, and a system seeks their common point, line, plane, or empty intersection.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use systems in three variables to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Eliminate one variable from two equation pairs, solve the reduced system, and back-substitute.

A contradiction means no solution; a free variable produces an infinite family.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through three-plane gallery, elimination ladder, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is performing elimination on only one pair and stopping with insufficient information.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Solve x+y+z=6,x-y+z=2,x+y-z=0.

**Solution.** (1,2,3).

**Interpretation.** Pairwise subtraction isolates variables.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Meaning 0=5.

**Solution.** No solution.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses systems in three variables in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Meaning free variable.

**Solution.** Infinitely many solutions.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A contradiction means no solution; a free variable produces an infinite family.

## P7.5. Matrix notation and operations

**Lesson outcome.** Represent rectangular arrays with matrices and perform dimension-compatible addition, scalar multiplication, and matrix multiplication.

### Opening

A matrix is a rectangular array whose dimensions record row and column counts.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use matrix notation and operations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Add corresponding entries, scale every entry, and multiply using row-column dot products when inner dimensions match.

Matrix multiplication is generally not commutative, and one order may be undefined.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through matrix anatomy, dimension compatibility, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is multiplying corresponding entries instead of using dot products.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Multiply [[1,2],[0,3]] by [[4],[5]].

**Solution.** [[14],[15]].

**Interpretation.** Each output entry is a row-column dot product.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Can 2x2 add 2x3?

**Solution.** No.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses matrix notation and operations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Dot (1,3) with (2,-1).

**Solution.** -1.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Matrix multiplication is generally not commutative, and one order may be undefined.

**P7.5-V2 - Dimension compatibility.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Add corresponding entries, scale every entry, and multiply using row-column dot products when inner dimensions match. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

## P7.6. Augmented matrices and row operations

**Lesson outcome.** Translate linear systems into augmented matrices and justify elementary row operations as equivalent-system transformations.

### Opening

An augmented matrix records a linear system in a fixed variable order.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use augmented matrices and row operations to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Include zero coefficients, translate each equation into a row, and use row swap, nonzero scaling, or row replacement to preserve the solution set.

Multiplying a row by zero is forbidden because it erases a constraint.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through system-to-matrix translation, row-operation meanings, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is changing coefficients without changing the constant term.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Row for x+3z=8 in x,y,z order.

**Solution.** [1,0,3|8].

**Interpretation.** The zero preserves the y-column.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Name row operations.

**Solution.** Swap, nonzero scale, row replacement.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses augmented matrices and row operations in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Why zero placeholders?

**Solution.** Preserve column alignment.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Multiplying a row by zero is forbidden because it erases a constraint.

### Figure storyboard

**P7.6-V1 - System-to-matrix translation.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: Row for x+3z=8 in x,y,z order. Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: [1,0,3|8].

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of system-to-matrix translation, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P7.6-V2 - Row-operation meanings.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: Include zero coefficients, translate each equation into a row, and use row swap, nonzero scaling, or row replacement to preserve the solution set. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

*Caption:* Row-operation meanings: visual evidence for translate linear systems into augmented matrices and justify elementary row operations as equivalent-system transformations.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of row-operation meanings, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P7.6-V3 - Forbidden zero scaling.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is changing coefficients without changing the constant term. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

## P7.7. Gaussian elimination and echelon form

**Lesson outcome.** Use forward elimination to reach row-echelon form and solve by back-substitution.

### Opening

Gaussian elimination creates row-echelon form by clearing entries below successive pivots.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use gaussian elimination and echelon form to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Choose a pivot, eliminate below it, move to the lower submatrix, and back-substitute from the final pivot row.

Contradiction rows mean no solution; zero rows may reveal free variables.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through echelon staircase, forward elimination stages, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is losing the row-operation audit trail in arithmetic clutter.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Interpret [[1,2|3],[0,0|5]].

**Solution.** No solution.

**Interpretation.** The second row is 0=5.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Where zero rows?

**Solution.** At bottom.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses gaussian elimination and echelon form in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Meaning [0,0|7].

**Solution.** No solution.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: Contradiction rows mean no solution; zero rows may reveal free variables.

## P7.8. Gauss-Jordan elimination and reduced row-echelon form

**Lesson outcome.** Reduce matrices to RREF and interpret pivots, free variables, rank, and consistency directly.

### Opening

Reduced row-echelon form has pivot ones that are the only nonzero entries in their columns.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use gauss-jordan elimination and reduced row-echelon form to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Continue elimination above pivots, identify basic and free variables, and use rank to classify consistent systems.

RREF is unique for a matrix.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through rref checklist, pivot-free map, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is calling a matrix reduced before clearing above pivots.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** Consistent 3-variable system with rank 2.

**Solution.** One free variable and infinitely many solutions.

**Interpretation.** Rank is smaller than the variable count.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Define rank.

**Solution.** Number of pivots.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses gauss-jordan elimination and reduced row-echelon form in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** Consistent 2 variables,rank2.

**Solution.** Unique solution.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: RREF is unique for a matrix.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of rref checklist, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## P7.9. Determinants, singularity, and inverse matrices

**Lesson outcome.** Compute and interpret determinants, identify singular matrices, and solve suitable systems with inverse matrices.

### Opening

The determinant of a square matrix detects invertibility and geometric area scaling.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use determinants, singularity, and inverse matrices to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

For [[a,b],[c,d]], compute ad-bc; nonzero means invertible and zero means singular.

The absolute determinant is the two-dimensional area scale, and a negative sign reverses orientation.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through determinant as area scale, singular collapse, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is attempting to divide by a zero determinant.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** det [[1,2],[2,4]].

**Solution.** 0; singular.

**Interpretation.** The rows are dependent and area collapses.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** det [[0,1],[1,0]].

**Solution.** -1.

**Interpretation.** This example expresses determinants, singularity, and inverse matrices in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** What nonzero determinant implies.

**Solution.** Invertible.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: The absolute determinant is the two-dimensional area scale, and a negative sign reverses orientation.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of determinant as area scale, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P7.9-V2 - Singular collapse.** Visualize the mechanism explicitly: For [[a,b],[c,d]], compute ad-bc; nonzero means invertible and zero means singular. The figure must show intermediate states or mappings, not merely the final formula.

## P7.10. Linear transformations and multivariable modeling

**Lesson outcome.** Interpret matrices as transformations of vectors and construct a complete multivariable model using equations, inequalities, and matrices.

### Opening

A matrix transformation sends the standard basis vectors to its columns, determining the image of every vector.

This matters because the rest of Precalculus will repeatedly use linear transformations and multivariable modeling to decide what a formula means, what a graph can show, and which conclusions remain valid.

### Before you begin

1. Solve equations and systems.
2. Interpret graphs as solution sets.
3. Use organized arithmetic and units.

### Exact learner exposition

Read the column images, express an input vector as a basis combination, and form the same combination of output columns.

A matrix model must keep rows, columns, units, and input meanings consistent.

A symbolic answer is not complete by itself. In this lesson, the same claim must also be readable through basis-vector explorer, transformation gallery, or another equivalent representation.

A complete response states what the result means. When the setting is contextual, include units and a meaningful domain. When the result is numerical, distinguish an exact value from an approximation and state the precision used.

### Common mistake

A frequent error is switching the row and column interpretations halfway through a model.

### Worked examples

#### Foundation example

**Problem.** A=[[2,1],[0,3]], apply to (4,-1).

**Solution.** (7,-3).

**Interpretation.** The output is four first columns minus one second column.

#### Representation example

**Problem.** Apply to (3,2).

**Solution.** (-2,3).

**Interpretation.** This example expresses linear transformations and multivariable modeling in a second form and should agree with the defining relationship stated above.

#### Transfer example

**Problem.** What transformation?

**Solution.** 90-degree counterclockwise rotation.

**Interpretation.** The transfer succeeds only when this condition remains visible: A matrix model must keep rows, columns, units, and input meanings consistent.

### Figure storyboard

**P7.10-V1 - Basis-vector explorer.** Depict the foundation problem exactly: A=[[2,1],[0,3]], apply to (4,-1). Show the quantities, axes, intervals, or algebraic objects needed to reach this conclusion: (7,-3).

*Caption:* Transformation gallery: visual evidence for interpret matrices as transformations of vectors and construct a complete multivariable model using equations, inequalities, and matrices.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of transformation gallery, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

**P7.10-V3 - Multivariable model pipeline.** Place the correct structure beside the invalid move described here: A frequent error is switching the row and column interpretations halfway through a model. Mark the first point where the conclusions diverge.

*Accessible description:* A text-equivalent account of multivariable model pipeline, including every marked point, interval, equation, direction, asymptote, transformation, and excluded case used to support the conclusion.

## Unit investigation

The investigation for Systems, Matrices, and Multivariable Models combines the unit methods in one multistage problem. It requires a structured entry version, a less-scaffolded version, a complete worked solution, an alternate valid strategy, and an extension that tests a model limitation.

## Unit review and mastery

The review mixes current-unit questions with retrieval from earlier units. The mastery assessment balances symbolic fluency, graph and table interpretation, explanation, error analysis, and modeling transfer.
